Requirements of other standards 12.1 Cash flows of discontinued operations 12.2 Cash flows arising from the exploration of mineral resources 12.3 Cash flows arising from interests in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates Appendix A - Index of Illustrations Appendix B - Defined terms Statement of cash flows Overview IAS 7 Statement of Cash Flows requires the provision of information about the historical changes in cash and cash equivalents of an entity by means of a statement of cash flows. IAS 7 requires that this statement of cash flows, which is a primary financial statement, classifies cash flows as operating, investing or financing. This chapter addresses the preparation of the statement of cash flows and the related disclosures required by IAS 7. The chapter addresses the practical issues that frequently arise when attempting to classify cash flows as operating, investing and financing, and includes a number of areas where judgement may be required. The chapter also discusses the definitions of cash and cash equivalents, and the difficulties commonly encountered when applying these definitions. What you need to know • IAS 7 requires all entities to prepare a statement of cash flows that reports changes in cash and cash equivalents in the period. • Cash flows must be classified as operating, investing or financing, having regard to the definitions in IAS 7, in a manner that is most appropriate to the business of the entity. • Only investments that are held to meet short term cash commitments can be classified as cash equivalents. • Non-cash transactions must be excluded from the statement of cash flows. • IAS 7 sets out additional disclosure requirements, including: • Information on changes in liabilities arising from financing activities • Details of components of cash and cash equivalents and the policy the entity adopts for determining the composition of cash and cash equivalents • The amount of significant cash and cash equivalent balances not available for use by the entity Statement of cash flows Introduction 1.1 Background IAS 7 is one of the older standards in IFRS accounting standards, having been issued in 1992. The standard specifies how entities report information about the historical changes in cash and cash equivalents and applies to all entities regardless of their business activities, including financial institutions. Modifications to IAS 7 since it was originally published have been limited resulting in a standard that is much shorter and more succinct than many new and revised IFRS accounting standards. It was last updated in 2023 to require entities to provide disclosures about supply chain financing arrangements. IAS 7 requires that inflows and outflows of cash and cash equivalents are classified and reported under three different headings being: • Operating activities • Investing activities • Financing activities [IAS 7.10] The resulting information in the statement of cash flows should provide insight into an entity’s ability to generate cash to fund operations, reinvest to maintain or expand operating capacity, repay debt and distribute dividends, and how it manages cash. The definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in the standard, which are covered in section 1.5 below, provide guidance on how to distinguish the cash flows by activity. The standard is accompanied by a number of illustrative examples. These illustrative examples are referred to, where relevant, throughout this chapter. 1.2 The statement of cash flows as a primary financial statement The statement of cash flows is a primary financial statement and must be presented with equal prominence to the statement of profit or loss and comprehensive income, the statement of financial position and the statement of changes in equity. [IAS 1.11]. Arguably, it provides some of the most important information in the financial statements, as all entities need cash to run their day-to-day operations, pay their obligations and provide returns to investors, regardless of their particular line of business or geographical location. This information, when combined with information in the rest of the financial statements, is useful in assessing factors that may affect the entity’s liquidity, financial flexibility, profitability, and risk. The Conceptual Framework for Financial Reporting (the Conceptual Framework) highlights the importance of information about cash flows in the reporting period as a means to help users understand a reporting entity’s operations, evaluate its financing and investing activities, assess its liquidity or solvency and interpret other information about financial performance. [CF 1.20]. This was rightly noted by the IFRS IC in its July 2014 agenda decision regarding the presentation and content of the condensed statement of cash flows in the interim financial statements according to IAS 34 Interim Financial Reporting. The agenda decision 1 noted that even a condensed statement of cash flows should include all information that is relevant in understanding the entity’s ability to generate cash flows and the entity’s needs to utilise these cash flows. Cash flow information also helps users to assess management’s stewardship of the entity’s economic resources. The Conceptual Framework guidance is further discussed in Chapter 2 at section 4.2. 1.3 Transparency and consistency of cash flow presentation In recent years, the statement of cash flows has become an area of scrutiny for investors as well as regulators from a number of jurisdictions, with both classification errors and insufficient disclosures highlighted as key areas of concern. This is partly because the use of cash flows as a key performance metric in entities’ communications to the financial markets has become increasingly common along with, and sometimes even in replacement of, other Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) more focused on the generation of income. IFRIC Update, July 2014, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows This is also because the definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in IAS 7 are broad and only illustrated by lists of examples which are not exhaustive. Whilst it is tempting to think that a statement of cash flows will be a simple matter of fact with less need for judgement around recognition and measurement of cash flows compared to other areas of the financial statements, the lack of clarity in the standard as to how some cash flows from common transactions are required to be categorised has resulted in diversity amongst preparers. Some common areas where judgement may be required are discussed in sections 3 and 4 below in reference to the definition of cash and cash equivalents and classification. In addition, the interaction of IAS 7 with new standards such as IFRS 9 Financial Instruments, IFRS 15 Revenue from Contracts with Customers and IFRS 16 Leases has led to further difficulties in applying the standard. The IFRS IC has received numerous requests for clarification on the requirements of the standard. However, many of these clarifications have never been finalised, leaving preparers and users faced with many practical difficulties, particularly in the application of the classification requirements of the standard. Some of the most common difficulties are discussed in the remainder of this chapter. The preparation of a cash flow statement is not merely a mechanical exercise. As a result, investors and regulators have focused on the adequacy of disclosures explaining how the requirements of IAS 7 have been applied. Where the appropriate classification of cash flows in the cash flow statement is not clear from IAS 7 and an entity has therefore applied judgement to determine the appropriate presentation of material items based on the principles of IAS 7, these judgements must be clearly disclosed. This is further discussed in section 4.4 below. 1.4 Future developments 1.4.1 IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial statements IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial Statements, which supersedes IAS 1 Presentation of Financial Statements for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027 (with earlier application permitted) was issued by the IASB in April 2024. The standard makes limited consequential amendments to the existing requirements of IAS 7. The amendments eliminate the current policy choice in IAS 7 for classification of cash flows from interest and dividends in the statement of cash flows. Dividends paid will be included in financing cash flows. Entities that do not have a specified main business activity of investing in assets or providing finance to customers will be required to classify interest paid as financing activities, and interest and dividends received as cash flows from investing activities. For entities that do have a specified main business activity of investing in assets or providing finance to customers, the classification of dividends received, interest paid and interest received will be impacted by the classification of the related income and expenses in the statement of profit or loss. When applying the indirect approach, IAS 7 is amended to require that the starting point for determining cash flows from operating activities is the operating profit or loss subtotal, which is a required subtotal in the income statement under IFRS 18. IFRS 18 and the impact on the statement of cash flows is discussed further in Chapter 4 at section 5.1. 1.4.2 IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures IFRS 19 Subsidiaries without Public Accountability: Disclosures was issued by the IASB in May 2024 to simplify the preparation of financial statements by eligible subsidiaries. Eligible subsidiaries that elect to apply IFRS 19 are allowed to provide reduced disclosures while still applying the recognition, measurement and presentation requirements in other IFRS accounting standards. IFRS 19 is effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027, with earlier application permitted (subject to jurisdictional endorsement). See Chapter 4 at section 7 for further details on the requirements of IFRS 19. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Statement of cash flows 1.4.3 Derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities settled through electronic transfers - amendments to IFRS 9 In September 2021, the IFRS IC was asked when a financial asset settled by a cash payment received via an electronic transfer system is derecognised. The discussion was extended to include the derecognition of a financial liability settled by a payment in the same scenario. The IFRS IC identified diversity in practice for the timing of derecognition of financial assets and financial liabilities, not just those settled via an electronic transfer system, but also using other methods. As a result of this, the IFRS IC decided to hand this matter over to the IASB who included this in the IFRS 9 PIR for Classification and Measurement. In May 2024, the IASB issued the Amendments to the Classification and Measurement of Financial Instruments, which included clarifications to the application guidance for derecognition of financial assets and liabilities as a response to the above. The amendments are effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2026, with early application allowed. # Chapter 48 at sections 3.5 and 6.1 the amendments are discussed in more detail. Chapter 48 at section 9.2 discusses the transitional requirements. 1.4.4 Other proposals In July 2022, the IASB issued a Feedback Statement on the Third Agenda Consultation, following publication of a Request for Information in March 2021. The Feedback Statement summarises the input received on the IASB’s six key activities and work plan for the period 2022 to 2026, and the IASB’s response to the comments and suggestions made by stakeholders. The statement communicated that the IASB intends to include in its research project pipeline for 2022 to 2026 a project on Statement of Cash Flows and Related Matters. As part of its initial work, the IASB will consider whether the project should aim to review IAS 7 comprehensively or, instead, to make more targeted improvements. 1.5 Terms used in IAS 7 Extract from IAS 7 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: Cash comprises cash on hand and demand deposits. Cash equivalents are short-term, highly liquid investments that are readily convertible to known amounts of cash and which are subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. Cash flows are inflows and outflows of cash and cash equivalents. Operating activities are the principal revenue-producing activities of the entity and other activities that are not investing or financing activities. Investing activities are the acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other investments not included in cash equivalents. Financing activities are activities that result in changes in the size and composition of the contributed equity and borrowings of the entity. **[IAS 7.6].** Statement of cash flows Objective and scope of IAS 7 2.1 Objective The objective of IAS 7 is to require entities to provide information about changes in cash and cash equivalents in the reporting period in a statement which classifies cash flows from operating, investing and financing activities. The standard aims to give users of financial statements a basis to evaluate the entity’s ability to generate cash and cash equivalents and its needs to utilise those cash flows. [IAS 7 Objective]. The standard explains that a statement of cash flows, in combination with the rest of the financial statements, provides information to users to enable them to evaluate the changes in net assets of an entity, its financial structure (including its liquidity and solvency) and its ability to affect the amounts and timing of cash flows to adapt to changing circumstances and opportunities. [IAS 7.4]. The historical cash flow information is often used as an indicator of the amount, timing and certainty of future cash flows. It is also useful in examining the relationship between profitability and net cash flow and the impact of changing prices. [IAS 7.5]. 2.2 Scope A statement of cash flows is required to make up a complete set of any financial statements in accordance with IFRS accounting standards. [IAS 1.10]. IAS 7 therefore applies to all entities, regardless of size, operations, ownership structure or industry, and includes wholly owned subsidiaries and banks, insurance entities and other financial institutions. There are no exemptions from the requirements of the standard. The standard argues that users of an entity’s financial statements are interested in how the entity generates and uses cash and cash equivalents regardless of the nature of its activities and irrespective of whether cash can be viewed as the product of the entity, as may be the case with a financial institution. All entities need cash to conduct their operations, to pay their obligations, and to provide returns to their investors. Accordingly, all entities are required to present a statement of cash flows. [IAS 7.3]. Statement of cash flows Defining cash and cash equivalents Since the objective of a statement of cash flows is to provide an analysis of changes in cash and cash equivalents, an understanding of the definition of cash and of cash equivalents is essential to its preparation. A statement of cash flows under IAS 7 excludes movements between items that are cash and cash equivalents because these are components of an entity’s cash management, rather than part of its operating, investing and financing activities. [IAS 7.9]. Amounts that fail to meet the definitions of cash and cash equivalents, for example due to restrictions (as in Illustration 3-2 in section 3.2.5 below), cannot be included as part of cash and cash equivalents for the purposes of preparing the statement of cash flows. In the statement of financial position, such amounts must be presented separately from cash and cash equivalents. Cash flows relating to restricted amounts that fail to meet the definition of cash and cash equivalents would generally meet the definition of investing activities, as they relate to the acquisition or disposal of investments (see section 4.2 below). Cash held in a foreign currency or investments acquired in a currency other than the functional currency of the entity (‘foreign currency’) are not excluded from qualifying as cash and cash equivalents merely because they are denominated in a foreign currency, provided they meet the other criteria discussed above. 3.1 Cash Extract from IAS 7 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: Cash comprises cash on hand and demand deposits. … **[IAS 7.6].** IAS 7 defines cash as ”cash on hand and demand deposits” and includes no other requirements with respect to determining whether an item qualifies as cash. This is in contrast to the definition of cash equivalents which, as explained in section 3.2 below, includes the requirement that cash equivalents are held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. In defining ‘cash’, IAS 7 does not explain what is meant by ‘demand deposits’, perhaps because the term is commonly understood as amounts that can be withdrawn on demand, without prior notice being required or a penalty being charged (for example, by an additional fee or forfeiture of interest). In contrast to ‘demand deposits’, term deposits are discussed in section 3.2.3 below. In practice, cash may be held by an entity for a specific purpose or may be subject to restrictions on its use, imposed either by the bank, by the entity or by a third party. The nature of any restriction on use of cash (and cash equivalents - see section 3.2.5 below) must be carefully assessed to determine if the balance still meets the definition of cash in the standard. 3.1.1 Restrictions on the use of cash In March 2022, the IFRS IC issued an agenda decision 3 following a request about whether an entity includes a demand deposit as a component of cash and cash equivalents in its statements of cash flows and financial position when the demand deposit is subject to contractual restrictions on use agreed with a third party. In the fact pattern described in the request, the entity: • Holds a demand deposit whose terms and conditions do not prevent the entity from accessing the amounts held in it (that is, were the entity to request any amount from the deposit, it would receive that amount on demand). • Has a contractual obligation with a third party to keep a specified amount of cash in that separate demand deposit and to use the cash only for specified purposes. If the entity were to use the amounts held in the demand deposit for purposes other than those agreed with the third party, the entity would be in breach of its contractual obligation. IFRIC Update, March 2022, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows The IFRS IC concluded that • Restrictions on the use of a demand deposit arising from a contract with a third party do not result in the deposit no longer being cash unless those restrictions change the nature of the deposit in a way that it would no longer meet the definition of cash in IAS 7. • In the fact pattern described in the request, the contractual restrictions on use of the amounts held in the demand deposit do not change the nature of the deposit - the entity can access those amounts on demand. The IFRS IC therefore concluded that the entity includes the demand deposit as a component of ‘cash and cash equivalents’ in its statement of cash flows. The IFRS IC also noted that: • IAS 1 requires the entity to disaggregate the cash and cash equivalents line item in the statement of financial position and present the demand deposit separately when this is relevant to an understanding of its financial position. [IAS 1.54, IAS 1.55]. • IAS 7 requires an entity to disclose the components of cash and cash equivalents: applying this requirement, in this fact pattern, the entity discloses the demand deposit as a component of cash and cash equivalents. [IAS 7.45]. • The entity must also consider whether to disclose additional information in the context of the requirements in IFRS 7 Financial Instruments: Disclosures about liquidity risk and how the entity manages that risk. • If the information provided in accordance with IAS 7 and IFRS 7 is insufficient to enable users of financial statements to understand the impact of the restrictions on the entity’s financial position, the entity considers whether additional disclosures are required. [IAS 1.31]. Where the restrictions on use do not arise from a separate contractual arrangement with a third party but are part of the terms and conditions directly relating to use of and access to the bank account so that cash cannot be withdrawn on demand the cash held in the bank account will fail to meet the definition of cash. However, it is possible that it may meet the definition of cash equivalents. Section 3.2 below discusses considerations relevant for the classification of a deposit other than cash as cash equivalent. The IFRS IC agenda decision discussed in this section does not address the classification of cash held by a subsidiary of a group that meets the definition of cash in respect of the subsidiary but for which use is restricted within the wider group, for example, cash and cash equivalents held by a subsidiary operating under exchange controls or other legal restrictions that prevent their general use by the parent or other subsidiaries. Section 11.1.4 below discusses the disclosure requirements that apply in these situations. 3.1.2 Cryptocurrencies vs. Central Bank Digital Currencies (CBDC) In a June 2019 agenda decision, the IFRS IC defined a cryptocurrency as a crypto-asset with all of the following characteristics: • a digital or virtual currency recorded on a distributed ledger that uses cryptography for security, • not issued by a jurisdictional authority or other party, and • does not give rise to a contract between the holder and another party. In the agenda decision the IFRS IC observed that cryptocurrencies did not have at that time the characteristics of cash. In particular, the IFRS IC noted that, although some cryptocurrencies could be used in exchange for particular goods or services, it was not aware of any cryptocurrency that was used as a medium of exchange or as the monetary unit in pricing goods or services to such an extent that it would have been the basis on which all transactions were measured and recognised in financial statements.4 Until now, the volume of transactions in which cryptocurrencies are used as a medium of exchange has remained quite low and some crypto-assets are not used as a medium of exchange at all at this stage. There are some jurisdictions where payment in bitcoin, for example, is accepted, but currently, the acceptance of cryptocurrencies by a merchant is not mandated in most jurisdictions. IFRIC Update, June 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows How we see it We are not aware of any cryptocurrencies that would currently be considered cash. In future periods, a holder would need to consider whether those cryptocurrencies represent a medium of exchange and unit of account to such extent that it could be the basis on which the holder recognises and measures all transactions in its financial statements (i.e., it could act as the functional currency of an entity). Cryptocurrencies also currently do not meet the definition of cash equivalents because they are generally not convertible to known amounts of cash, nor are they subject to an insignificant risk of change in value or have maturities. Cryptocurrencies are discussed in more detail in Chapter 18 at section 11.6 and in our Applying IFRS: Accounting by holders of crypto assets.5 In contrast, many governments and central banks worldwide are exploring the possibility of using government-backed digital currencies. Central Bank Digital Currencies (CBDC) are issued and regulated by a nation’s monetary authority or central bank. They are the digital form of a country’s fiat currency. For example, in October 2021, the Central Bank of Nigeria (CBN) issued guidelines for the operation of a permissioned blockchain based CBDC named ’eNaira’. As per the Guidelines, eNaira is the digital form of the Nigerian fiat currency (Naira), issued by the CBN at par with the physical Naira (that is 1:1), as a direct liability of the CBN and as legal tender and part of the currency circulation. CBN exclusively can mint, distribute, redeem and destroy eNaira. How we see it Unlike cryptocurrencies discussed above, we believe that a Central Bank Digital Currency would normally be considered as cash because it is the digital form of a country’s fiat currency. This is because it is issued by a central bank, as a direct liability of that central bank and as legal tender in the jurisdiction of that Central bank recognised as a medium of exchange in the country of issuance, where prices are expressed in the local currency, of which the CBDC is the digital version. 3.1.3 Client money Some financial institutions and other entities hold money on behalf of clients. The terms on which such money is held can vary widely. This is discussed in more detail in Chapter 48 at section 8. There are numerous types of arrangements that result in an entity holding client money, and conversely result in an entity depositing their money in a client account held by another entity. It is impossible to generalise as to the appropriate treatment in either of the parties’ financial statement as this will be dependent on a careful assessment of the terms and conditions of each specific arrangement. However, as explained in Chapter 48 at section 8, there are a number of factors that must be considered. From the perspective of the depositary entity (i.e., the entity that holds the money on behalf of its client) it is important to identify precisely how the funds are held. Are they required to be segregated in bank accounts separate from that of the depositary entity itself or are they allowed to be commingled with those of the depositary entity? Other key considerations include: • Which party is at risk from the failure of assets, such as bank accounts, in which the client money is held? • The status of the funds in the event of the insolvency of either the reporting entity or its client • Whether the reporting entity can use the cash for its own purposes as opposed to administering the cash on behalf of the client in its capacity as an agent And • Which party has the benefit of income from the assets If the depositary entity assesses that the funds will be included as an asset on its statement of financial position, it would then need to assess what kind of financial asset this represents; cash or cash equivalents, by applying the definitions in IAS 7. A corresponding liability would also be recognised. Any restrictions on the use of the cash or cash equivalents must be considered and disclosed as explained in section 11.1 below. From the perspective of the entity depositing its money in a client account held by another entity, it would be necessary to consider whether the funds still meet the definition of cash (see section 3.1 above). This would only be the case if the funds are available to withdraw on demand, and without penalty, directly by the entity. [IAS 7.6]. If the funds held are subject to an advance notice period before they can be withdrawn or used, or there are other restrictions on their withdrawal, it is unlikely that they will meet the definition of cash. Applying IFRS, Accounting by holders of crypto-assets: Updated October 2021, EY, October 2021, available on ey.com/IFRS. Statement of cash flows Depending on the specific facts and circumstances, the funds may meet the definition of a ‘cash equivalent’ however, this would only be the case if the definition of cash equivalents is met, including the requirement that the purpose of the funds is to meet short-term cash commitments of the entity as explained in section 3.2 below. If the definitions of cash and cash equivalents are not met, the funds will be presented as a receivable in the financial statements of the entity depositing its money in a client account. 3.2 Cash equivalents Extract from IAS 7 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: … Cash equivalents are short-term, highly liquid investments that are readily convertible to known amounts of cash and which are subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. … Cash equivalents are held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. For an investment to qualify as a cash equivalent it must be readily convertible to a known amount of cash and be subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. Therefore, an investment normally qualifies as a cash equivalent only when it has a short maturity of, say, three months or less from the date of acquisition. Equity investments are excluded from cash equivalents unless they are, in substance, cash equivalents, for example in the case of preferred shares acquired within a short period of their maturity and with a specified redemption date. **[IAS 7.6, IAS 7.7].** It is important to understand the reporting entity’s cash management policies, especially when considering whether balances that are not obviously cash on hand and demand deposits would be classified as cash equivalents, as the standard states that cash equivalents are held to meet short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. [IAS 7.7]. Cash management includes the investment of cash in excess of immediate needs into cash equivalents, [IAS 7.9], such as short-term investments. For investments to qualify as a cash equivalent, they must be: • Short term • Highly liquid • Readily convertible into known amounts of cash And • Subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value [IAS 7.6] However, the purpose for which the investments are held must also be determined. Where investments meet the four criteria above but are not held by the entity to meet short term cash commitments, they cannot be classified as cash equivalents. As discussed below, careful consideration of facts and circumstances and judgement may be needed to determine whether an investment meets the definition of cash equivalents. 3.2.1 Short-term investments Cash equivalents are held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. For an investment to qualify as a cash equivalent it must be readily convertible to a known amount of cash and be subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. The standard argues that, normally, only an investment with a short maturity of, say, three months or less from the date of acquisition qualifies as a cash equivalent - see section 3.2.3 below. Equity investments are excluded unless they are cash equivalents in substance. IAS 7 provides an example of such an instrument, being redeemable preference shares acquired within a short period of their maturity and with a specified redemption date. [IAS 7.7]. The IFRS IC considered, in July 2009,6 what the standard means when it refers to a ‘known amount of cash’ and confirmed that the amount of cash that will be received must be known at the time of the initial investment. Accordingly, traded commodities, such as gold bullion, would not be eligible for inclusion in cash equivalents because the proceeds to be realised from such an investment is determined at the date of disposal rather than being known or determinable when the investment is made. IFRIC Update, July 2009, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows 3.2.2 Money market funds Entities commonly invest in money market-type funds such as an open-ended mutual fund that invests in money market instruments like certificates of deposit, commercial paper, treasury bills, bankers’ acceptances and repurchase agreements. Although there are different types of money market funds, they generally aim to provide investors with low-risk, low-return investment while preserving the value of the assets and maintaining a high level of liquidity. The question then arises as to whether investments in such funds can be classified as cash equivalents. As discussed below, entities may consider the underlying investments of the fund when assessing the significance of the risk of changes in value, however when determining whether classification as a cash equivalent is appropriate, the unit of account is the investment in the money market fund itself. To meet the definition of a cash equivalent, an investment must be short term; highly liquid; readily convertible to a known amount of cash; and subject to insignificant risk of changes in value. IAS 7 considers a maturity date of three months or less from the date of acquisition to be short-term in the context of maturity, however money market funds generally do not have a legal maturity date. Instead, if the investments are puttable and can be sold back to the fund at any time, they meet the short-term criterion. Typically, investments in money market funds are redeemed directly with the fund, therefore in assessing liquidity of the investment, focus must be on the liquidity of the fund itself. In making this assessment, entities must consider the nature of any redemption restrictions in place as these may prevent the redemption of the investment in the short term. Alternatively, if a money market fund investment is quoted in an active market, it might be regarded as highly liquid on that basis. The short-term and highly liquid investment must also be readily convertible into known amounts of cash and be subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. [IAS 7.6]. As discussed in section 3.2.1 above, this means that the amount of cash that will be received must be known at the time of the initial investment. Accordingly, investments in shares or units of money market funds cannot be considered as cash equivalents simply because they are convertible at any time at the then market price in an active market. The IFRS IC also confirmed that an entity would have to satisfy itself that any investment was subject to an insignificant risk of change in value for it to be classified as a cash equivalent.7 How we see it In our view, in order for an entity to satisfy itself that an investment in a money market fund is subject to an insignificant risk of change in value, the entity must conclude that the range of possible returns is very small. This evaluation is first made at the time of acquiring the investment and reassessment is required if the facts and circumstances change. The evaluation will involve consideration of factors such as: • the maturity of the underlying investments of the fund • the credit rating of the fund; the nature of the investments held by the fund (i.e., not subject to volatility) • the extent of diversification in the portfolio (which is expected to be very high) • the investment policy of the fund And • any mechanisms by the fund to guarantee returns (for example by reference to short-term money market interest rates) Investments are often held for purposes other than to act as a ready store of value that can be quickly converted into cash when needed to meet short-term cash commitments. It is therefore important, even where the criteria above are met, to understand the purpose of an investment in a particular money market fund. Where an investment otherwise satisfies the criteria in IAS 7.6 but is not held by the entity to satisfy short-term cash commitments, it must not be classified as a cash equivalent. [IAS 7.7]. Substantial judgement may be required in assessing whether an investment in money market funds can be classified as a cash equivalent. Therefore, appropriate disclosure is essential. As discussed in section 11 below, entities are required to disclose the composition of cash equivalents and, where relevant, this will include significant judgements made in classifying investments in money market funds. IFRIC Update, July 2009, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows 3.2.3 Investments with maturities greater than three months The longer the term of an investment, the greater the risk that a change in market conditions (such as interest rates) can significantly affect its value. For this reason, IAS 7 excludes most equity investments from cash equivalents and restricts the inclusion of other investments to those with a short maturity (which the standard illustrates as three months or less from the date of their acquisition by the entity). [IAS 7.7]. An investment with a term on acquisition of, say, nine months is not reclassified as a cash equivalent from the date on which there is less than three months remaining to its maturity. This is because the classification as cash equivalent is based on the maturity and the nature of the instrument at acquisition. The fact that the instrument is now approaching its maturity does not change its nature nor the purpose for which it was acquired. In the final analysis, cash equivalents are held to meet short-term cash commitments and amounts can only be included in cash equivalents if they can be regarded as being nearly as accessible as cash and essentially as free from exposure to changes in value as cash. For example, an entity might justify including in cash equivalents a fixed deposit with an original term longer than three months if it effectively functions like a demand deposit. Typically, a fixed deposit will carry a penalty charge for withdrawal prior to maturity. A penalty will usually indicate that the investment is held for investment purposes rather than the purpose of meeting short-term cash needs. However, some fixed deposits still offer interest at a prevailing demand deposit rate in the event of early withdrawal, with any penalty limited to the entity being required to forego the incremental higher interest that it would have received if the deposit were held to maturity. In this case, it may be arguable that there is effectively no significant penalty for early withdrawal, as the entity receives at least the same return that it otherwise would have in a demand deposit arrangement. How we see it In our view, investments in deposit accounts that upon withdrawal are subject to penalties of such significance, that the funds obtained are appreciably different than the funds that would have been obtained had a shorter-term investment been initially purchased, are not consistent with the assertion that the investment was held to meet short-term cash needs. Significance is viewed from the perspective of the entity, taking into consideration the environment in which it operates. If the investor would be indifferent to the penalty because the funds obtained are relatively consistent with the funds that would have been obtained had a shorter-term investment been initially purchased, the terms of the instrument are consistent with the assertion that the investment was held for meeting short-term cash needs. This can be seen in the following Illustration: Illustration 3-1: Investments with a maturity of greater than three months Entity A places funds with a bank in a fixed deposit with a stated maturity date of six months. The features of the fixed deposit are as follows: • If held to maturity, the deposit bears interest at a fixed six-month rate (FD rate) determined at the date of deposit. The FD rate is significantly higher than the interest rates offered for a typical demand deposit account (DD rate). The DD rate is significantly higher than zero. • Interest accrues on a daily basis based on the FD rate. • The entity can redeem the fixed deposit at any time before the maturity date. When redeemed, the deposit must be redeemed in full. The entity may not increase the deposit amount subsequent to the initial investment. A penalty charge in the form of forfeited interest is imposed upon early redemption (see Scenario 1 and Scenario 2 that illustrate common terms used to calculate the penalty charge). • The entity asserts that the investment may be used to meet short-term cash commitments. Scenario 1 If the entity redeems the fixed deposit at any time before the maturity date, the entity will not be entitled to any interest. The entity will only receive the original principal amount. Scenario 2 If the entity redeems the fixed deposit at any time before the maturity date, the entity is entitled to interest based on the typical DD rate from the initial deposit date to the date of redemption plus the original principal amount. The balance (if any) of the interest at the FD rate in excess of the DD rate is forfeited as an early withdrawal penalty. In both scenarios 1 and 2 above, the principal amount is always redeemed in full. Statement of cash flows Analysis The conclusion depends on the facts and circumstances. Whether the deposit meets the definition of a cash equivalent is a matter of judgement. The principle is that to be a cash equivalent, the entity must hold the investment for meeting short-term cash needs. Scenario 1 The fixed deposit has a stated maturity period from the date of deposit but allows the entity to redeem the fixed deposit immediately; accordingly, the instrument could be viewed as short-term and readily convertible. However, in this scenario, the penalty results in the entity earning no interest. If the deposit had been placed in a demand deposit account rather than in the fixed deposit, the interest earned would have been significantly higher than zero, as set out in the fact pattern. This represents an economic penalty which must be evaluated to determine whether it creates a sufficiently large disincentive such that the entity would not withdraw the funds before the maturity date. This assessment may be subject to some degree of judgement based on facts and circumstances. If such a large disincentive exists, this would be inconsistent with the assertion that the investment is held for meeting short-term cash needs and the fixed deposit would not meet the definition of a cash equivalent. Scenario 2 The fixed deposit is similar to an account with a one-day demand deposit interest rate that earns ‘bonus’ interest if the deposit is maintained until a specified date (maturity). The potential bonus interest that is forfeited upon early withdrawal is not viewed as a penalty, because the rate of interest received is consistent with management’s investment purpose to meet short-term cash commitments rather than an investment to be held until the maturity date. Therefore, the fixed deposit qualifies as a cash equivalent. Even if the DD rate is not significantly higher than zero, as long as it is not negative, the interest received on early redemption is still consistent with management’s investment purpose to meet short-term cash commitments and the fixed deposit qualifies as a cash equivalent. In scenario 2 in the above example, the investment is classified as a cash equivalent because it meets all criteria and is held for meeting short-term cash needs. A consequence of this classification is that the calculation of the effective interest rate (EIR) in accordance with IFRS 9 must be done on a consistent basis. IFRS 9 requires that, when calculating the EIR, all contractual terms of the financial instrument, for example prepayments, call and similar options, are considered. See Chapter 46 at section 3.4. [IFRS 9 Appendix A]. In this example, it is implicit in the classification that the entity may need to withdraw the deposit to meet short-term cash commitments and, as a consequence, will only receive interest at the (lower) demand deposit rate. Therefore, the EIR must reflect this intention. 3.2.4 Bank overdrafts Although bank borrowings are generally considered to be financing activities, there are circumstances in which bank overdrafts repayable on demand are included as a component of cash and cash equivalents. This is only in cases where bank overdrafts are repayable on demand and the use of short-term overdrafts form an integral part of an entity’s cash management practices. Evidence supporting the latter assertion would be that the bank balance often fluctuates from being positive to overdrawn. [IAS 7.8]. The IFRS IC observed that where overdrafts do not often fluctuate from being negative to positive, this is an indicator that the arrangement does not form part of an entity’s cash management and, instead, represents a form of financing.8 3.2.5 Restrictions on the use of cash equivalents The nature of any restriction on the use of each item classified as cash equivalents must be carefully assessed to determine whether the restrictions affect the ability to meet the definition of cash equivalents in the standard. As discussed in section 3.2 above, for investments to qualify as a cash equivalent, they must be short term, highly liquid, readily convertible into known amounts of cash and subject to insignificant risk of changes in value. [IAS 7.6]. However, the purpose for which the investments are held must also be considered and this is a matter of facts and circumstances. Where investments meet the four criteria above but are not held by the entity to meet short term cash commitments, they cannot be classified as cash equivalents. Therefore, any restrictions on the use of an investment, whether arising directly from the contractual terms and conditions of the investment or arising from a separate contractual arrangement with a third party, that prevent the IFRIC Update, June 2018, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows investment being used to meet short term cash commitments, would result in the investment failing to meet the definition of a cash equivalent. Illustration 3-2: Investment which is not held to meet short term cash commitments Entity B has in issue loan notes with a remaining 2 years until maturity. One of the provisions of the financing agreement is that Entity B maintains CU30 million minimum cash balance in a separate bank account continuously until the loan notes are fully redeemed. If the balance on the relevant bank account falls below CU30 million, Entity B is obliged to notify a breach of contract, which triggers certain penalties and could, in certain circumstances, constitutes an early redemption event. Entity B chooses to deposit the CU30 million in a 60-day fixed maturity deposit account. Analysis The fixed deposit does not meet the definition of cash as it is not cash on hand and is not held in a demand deposit. [IAS 7.6]. The stated maturity period from the date of deposit is less than 3 months; accordingly, the instrument could be viewed as short-term and highly liquid. In addition, the nature of the bank deposit is that it is readily convertible into known amounts of cash and subject to insignificant risk of changes in value. However, the fixed deposit will not be used by Entity B to meet short term cash commitments, as the funds are being held to meet the provisions of a financing agreement which extends for a period of 2 years. Therefore, the cash in the account does not meet the definition of cash equivalents. The requirement to consider the purpose for which the investment is held arises as a result of paragraph 7 of IAS 7 which states that cash equivalents are held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. [IAS 7.7]. This can be contrasted with the definition of cash (see section 3.1 above) which does not contain any requirement to consider how or when the cash will be used and therefore ignores any restrictions on the use of cash arising from a contract with a third party. See section 3.1.1 above for a discussion on the classification of cash subject to restrictions. 3.3 Reassessment of cash and cash equivalents after initial recognition There is no specific requirement in IAS 7 for the re-assessment of the criteria for classification as cash or cash equivalents after initial recognition. However, it is implicit in the definitions in IAS 7 that the amounts must still meet those definitions at the end of each reporting period to be presented as such. Changes that may affect the criteria for classification could arise from contractual or non-contractual changes. For contractual changes, it will usually be clear when these changes are made and what the nature of the changes are. Such changes could affect the nature of the asset and require the new asset to be tested against the definition of cash and cash equivalents. Other changes in facts and circumstances could also require careful consideration and the application of judgement. For cash equivalents, reassessment may be particularly relevant when looking at the requirements that cash equivalents are highly liquid and subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. For example, since initial recognition of an investment as a cash equivalent, there may have been adverse changes to the credit status of the counterparty to the investment or a significant decline in the economic stability of the jurisdiction in which the funds are invested. At the end of the reporting period, the investment may no longer meet the definition of cash equivalents since it is no longer highly liquid and readily convertible into known amounts of cash and is now subject to more than an insignificant risk of changes in value. However, as discussed in section 3.2.3 above, an investment with a term on acquisition of, say, nine months is not reclassified as a cash equivalent from the date on which there is less than three months remaining to its maturity. This is because the classification as cash equivalent is based on the maturity and the nature of the instrument at acquisition. The fact that the instrument is now approaching its maturity does not change its nature nor the purpose for which it was acquired. 3.4 Current or non-current classification In respect of classification as current or non-current, for entities that present current and non-current assets and liabilities in the statement of financial position, IAS 1 requires that cash and cash equivalents are classified as current “unless the asset is restricted from being exchanged or used to settle a liability for at least twelve months after the reporting period.” [IAS 1.66(d)]. Statement of cash flows As discussed in section 3.1.1 above, in March 2022, the IFRS IC issued an agenda decision concluding that restrictions on the use of a demand deposit arising from a contract with a third party do not result in the deposit no longer being cash, unless those restrictions change the nature of the deposit in a way that it would no longer meet the definition of cash in IAS 7. This raises the question as to how the requirements of IAS 1 paragraph 66(d) apply to cash subject to such restrictions. However, the submission which gave rise to the agenda decision did not ask the IFRS IC to consider how the requirements of IAS 1.66(d) are applied and, the IFRS IC did not conclude on whether the demand deposit in the fact pattern would be classed as current or non-current. In respect of cash equivalents, the application of IAS 1.66(d) is far more straightforward, given that paragraph 7 of IAS 7 states that “Cash equivalents are held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes.” How we see it In our view, cash equivalents will always be classified as current. This is because cash equivalents must be held for the purpose of meeting short-term cash commitments rather than for investment or other purposes. Any restrictions that prevent the asset from being exchanged or used to settle a liability for at least 12 months after the reporting period would result in the asset failing to meet the definition of cash equivalents. Classification in the statement of cash flows Extract from IAS 7 The statement of cash flows shall report cash flows during the period classified by operating, investing and financing activities. **[IAS 7.10].** This classification is intended to allow users to assess the impact of these three types of activity on the financial position of the entity and the amount of its cash and cash equivalents. Whilst not stated explicitly in the standard, the presentation of operating, investing and financing cash flows usually follows this sequence in practice, and a total net cash flow for each standard heading must be shown. Comparative figures are required for all items in the statement of cash flows and the related notes. [IAS 1.38]. The components of cash flows are classified as operating, investing or financing activities in a manner which is most appropriate to the business of the entity. [IAS 7.11]. For example, the purchase of investments is likely to be classified as an operating cash flow for a financial institution, but as an investing cash flow for a manufacturer. Additionally, a single transaction may comprise elements of differently classified cash flows. For example, when repayments on a loan include both interest and capital, the element reflecting the interest expense may be included in either operating activities or financing activities (see section 4.4.1 below) whereas the capital repayment must be classified as a financing cash flow. [IAS 7.12]. Statement of cash flows A format of the statement of cash flows is illustrated in Practical example 4-1 below. As permitted by the standard, AstraZeneca PLC has included interest paid under operating activities, interest received under investing activities and dividends paid under financing activities. Practical example 4-1: AstraZeneca PLC (2023) United Kingdom AstraZeneca Annual Report & Form 20-F Information 2023 [extract] Financial Statement [extract] Statement of cash flows Having reviewed a variety of requests received from constituents for further guidance on the classification of cash flows, the IFRS IC and the IASB have reiterated the general requirement set out in IAS 7.11 that the primary principle for classification of cash flows should be in accordance with the nature of the activity in a manner that is most appropriate to the business of the entity.9 However, at its March 2013 meeting, the IFRS IC discussed how the definitions of operating, investing and financing cash flows in IAS 7 could be made clearer and thus could lead to a more consistent application of the primary principle but concluded that clarifying the application of the primary principle was a matter that was too broad for the IFRS IC to address.10 As a result, pending any change to the standard, there is likely to be some diversity in practice on how some common transactions are presented in the statement of cash flows. 4.1 Cash flows from operating activities Operating activities are defined as “the principal revenue-producing activities of the entity and other activities that are not investing or financing activities”. [IAS 7.6]. The standard states that the value of information on operating cash flows is twofold. It provides a key indicator of the extent to which the entity has generated sufficient cash flows from its operations to repay debt, pay dividends and make investments to maintain and increase its operating capability, without recourse to external sources of financing. Also, information about the components of historical operating cash flows may assist in the process of forecasting future operating cash flows, when used in conjunction with other financial statement information. [IAS 7.13]. Cash flows from operating activities generally result from transactions and other events that enter into the determination of profit or loss. Examples include: [IAS 7.14] • Cash receipts from the sale of goods and the rendering of services • Cash receipts from royalties, fees, commissions and other revenue • Cash payments to suppliers for goods and services • Cash payments to and on behalf of employees • Cash payments or refunds of income taxes unless they can be specifically identified with financing and investing activities • Cash receipts and payments from contracts held for dealing or trading purposes (see section 4.4.12 below regarding the allocation of cash flows paid or received under derivative contracts) Operating activities is also a ‘default category’ for any cash flows that do not meet the criteria of investing or financing cash flows. For example, as discussed in section 9.3.1 below, acquisition-related costs in a business combination that must be recognised as an expense, [IFRS 3.53], would also be classified as operating cash flows because there is no related asset that would justify classification as an investing cash flow. [IAS 7.16]. The nature of the business conducted by the entity will be a relevant factor in determining the appropriate classification of cash flows as operating, investing or financing. For example, when an entity holds securities and loans for dealing or trading purposes, they are similar to inventory acquired specifically for resale. Therefore, any related cash flows are classified as operating activities. Similarly, cash advances and loans made by financial institutions are usually classified as operating activities, since they relate to the main revenue-generating activity of that entity (see section 10.1 below). [IAS 7.15]. The proceeds from the sale of property, plant and equipment, which are usually included in cash flows from investing activities, are an example of an item that enters into the determination of profit or loss that is not usually an operating cash flow. [IAS 7.14]. However, the proceeds from sales of assets previously held for rental purposes are classified as cash flows from operating activities if the entity routinely sells such assets in its ordinary course of business. Similarly, cash payments to manufacture or acquire property, plant and equipment held for rental to others, and that are routinely sold in the ordinary course of business after rental, are also classified as cash flows from operating activities (see section 4.4.7 below). [IAS 7.14]. Cash flows from operating activities may be reported on a gross or net basis, also known as the direct and indirect methods. [IAS 7.18]. The use of the direct or indirect method is an accounting policy choice and must be applied consistently from period to period. Any change in method must be reported under IAS 8 Accounting Policies, Changes in Accounting Estimates and Errors. This would require comparatives to be restated and additional disclosures given, including the reasons for the change in policy. [IAS 8.29]. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Statement of cash flows 4.1.1 The direct method Under the direct method, major classes of gross cash receipts and gross cash payments are disclosed. [IAS 7.18]. IAS 7 encourages entities to use the direct method, because it provides information which may be useful in estimating future cash flows and which is not available under the indirect method. [IAS 7.19]. Under the direct method, information about major classes of gross cash receipts and payments may be obtained either: (a) From the accounting records of the entity Or (b) By adjusting sales, cost of sales (interest and similar income and interest expenses and similar charges for a financial institution) and other items in the statement of comprehensive income for changes during the period in inventories and operating receivables and payables, other items for which the cash effects are investing or financing cash flows and other non-cash items [IAS 7.19] A statement of cash flows prepared under the direct method must include the same disclosures of gross cash receipts and gross cash payments irrespective of which approach has been used to determine their value. There is no requirement for entities using the approach described in (b) above to present a reconciliation showing the adjustments made between, for example, revenue in the statement of comprehensive income and cash receipts from customers. Telefonica, S.A. is an example of an entity using the direct method for presenting its cash flows from operating activities, as illustrated in Practical example 4-2 below. Practical example 4-2: Telefónica, S.A. (2023) Spain Consolidated Annual Report 2023 [extract] Consolidated financial statements 2023 [extract] … 4.1.2 The indirect method The indirect method arrives at the same value for net cash flows from operating activities but does so by working back from amounts reported in the statement of comprehensive income. There are two approaches for presenting the net cash flows from operating activities when using the indirect method. The most common approach adjusts reported profit or loss for the effects of: (a) Changes in inventories and operating receivables and payables during the period (b) Non-cash items such as depreciation, provisions, deferred taxes, unrealised foreign currency gains and losses, and undistributed profits of associates And (c) All other items for which the cash effects are investing or financing cash flows [IAS 7.20] Statement of cash flows Woodside Energy Group Ltd has used this adjusted profit approach to present its indirect method statement of cash flows, as illustrated in Practical example 4-3 below. Practical example 4-3: Woodside Energy Group Ltd (2023) Australia ## ANNUAL REPORT [extract] FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] CONSOLIDATED STATEMENT OF CASH FLOWS [extract] … Alternatively, the indirect method of presentation can show separately revenues and expenses, adjusted for non-cash, investing or financing items, making up operating profit before working capital changes. [IAS 7.20]. An example of this rarely used alternative is given at the end of Example A in the Illustrative Examples accompanying IAS 7. [IAS 7.IE Example A] 4.1.2.A Starting point for the reconciliation When an entity adjusts reported profit or loss under the indirect method, a question arises as to which measure of profit or loss to use as a starting point for the reconciliation. IAS 7 does not define ‘profit or loss’. The term is defined in IAS 1 paragraph 7 as “Profit or loss is the total of income less expenses, excluding the components of other comprehensive income.” Paragraph 88 of IAS 1 refers to the profit and loss for the period as containing “all items of income and expense recognised in a period …” which might suggest that profit or loss after tax (as in Practical example 4-3 above) must be used. However, Example A in the Illustrative Examples accompanying IAS 7 illustrates the indirect method by showing profit before tax as a starting point (as is also shown in Practical example 4-1 above). [IAS 7.IE Example A]. How we see it In our view, profit before tax and profit after tax can both be used as the starting point for the reconciliation. We also observe in practice that there may be other profit measures currently used such as operating profit or similar. In our view, it is acceptable for an operating result sub-total to be used as a starting point for the reconciliation provided that it is a clearly identified sub-total on the face of the income statement, is reconciled to profit before or after tax on the face of the income statement and is representative of all the operating activities of the entity. In our view, alternative performance measures such as profit or loss before non-recurring operating items would not be acceptable starting points for the reconciliation. Statement of cash flows Entities should take into consideration the views of local regulators who make take a stricter view of this matter. It is relevant to note that IFRS 18 (which is mandatory for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027) makes consequential amendments to IAS 7 to require that the starting point for determining cash flows from operating activities is the operating profit or loss subtotal. This operating profit or loss subtotal is defined in IFRS 18 and will be a required subtotal in the statement of financial performance. See Chapter 4 at section 5.1. In the Basis for Conclusions to these amendments, the IASB sets out its rationale for this decision, explaining that an entity using operating profit or loss needs to present fewer adjustments to the starting point, which simplifies the presentation of the operating cash flows category. Fewer adjustments are needed because, compared to profit or loss, operating profit or loss includes fewer items of income and expenses for which the cash effects are classified as investing or financing cash flows. In addition, the difference between cash flows from operating activities and operating profit or loss provides a measure of operating accruals which some users may find useful. [IAS 7(2027).BC47]. 4.1.2.B Adjusting for changes in working capital To obtain the information on working capital movements for the indirect method, the figures in the statement of financial position must be analysed according to the three standard headings of the statement of cash flows. Thus, the reconciliation of profit or loss to cash flow from operating activities will include the increase or decrease in receivables or payables, but only in respect of those elements thereof that relate to operating activities. For example, amounts owed in respect of the acquisition of property, plant and equipment (other than assets held for rental and subsequent sale), intangible assets, or non-operational investments will be excluded from the movement in payables included in this reconciliation. Furthermore, when a group has acquired a subsidiary during the year, the change in working capital items will have to be split between the increase due to the acquisition (to the extent that the purchase consideration was settled in cash, this will be shown under investing activities) and the element related to post-acquisition operating activities which will be shown in the reconciliation. Further considerations needed when preparing a statement of cash flows for a group are explained in section 9 below. 4.2 Cash flows from investing activities Investing activities are defined as “the acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other investments not included in cash equivalents”. [IAS 7.6]. This separate category of cash flows allows users of the financial statements to understand the extent to which expenditures have been made for resources intended to generate future income and cash flows. Cash flows arising from investing activities include: (a) Payments to acquire, and receipts from the sale of, property, plant and equipment, intangibles and other long-term assets (including payments and receipts relating to capitalised development costs and self-constructed property, plant and equipment). (b) Payments to acquire, and receipts from the sale of, equity or debt instruments of other entities and interests in jointly controlled entities (other than payments and receipts for those instruments considered to be cash equivalents or those held for dealing or trading purposes). (c) Advances and loans made to, and repaid by, other parties (other than advances and loans made by a financial institution). And (d) Payments for, and receipts from, futures contracts, forward contracts, option contracts and swap contracts, except when the contracts are held for dealing or trading purposes, or the cash flows are classified as financing activities (see section 4.4.12 below regarding allocation of cash flows on derivative contracts). [IAS 7.16]. As part of its annual improvement project, the IASB added in 2008 that only expenditures that result in a recognised asset in the statement of financial position are eligible for classification as investing activities. [IAS 7.16]. Therefore, cash flows relating to costs recognised as an expense cannot be classified within investing activities. As a result, payments including those for exploration and evaluation activities and for research and development that are recognised as an asset are classified as investing cash flows, while entities that recognise such expenditures as an expense would classify the related payments as operating cash flows. Statement of cash flows This requirement was added in response to submissions made to the IFRS IC about the classification of expenditure incurred with the aim of generating future revenues, but that may not always result in the recognition of an asset. Examples included exploration and evaluation expenditure, advertising and promotional activities, staff training, and research and development. [IAS 7.BC3]. The IASB believes this approach aligns the classification of investing cash flows with the presentation in the statement of financial position, reduces divergence in practice and, therefore, results in financial statements that are easier for users to understand. [IAS 7.BC7]. It does not seem unreasonable that recurrent expenditure on items such as research and expensed mining costs are classified as operating cash flows. However, application to other items that do not give rise to an asset in the statement of financial position, such as acquisition-related costs (discussed in section 9.3.1 below) and the settlement of contingent consideration in a business combination (discussed in section 9.3.3 below) may prove to be more complicated. The IFRS IC and the IASB have discussed the application of this requirement in practice, as some users appear to have given precedence to the classification of cash flows consistently with the classification of the related item in the statement of financial position, [IAS 7.BC7], (sometimes referred to as the ‘cohesiveness principle’) over the objective in the standard to classify cash flows in accordance with the nature of the activity giving rise to the cash flow. [IAS 7.11]. In its discussion, the IASB agreed that the primary principle for the classification of cash flows should be in accordance with the nature of the activity, and that the requirement for the recognition of an asset should be read as a constraint on the application of the primary principle, rather than as a competing principle.11 Major classes of gross receipts and gross payments arising from investing activities are reported separately, except for those items that IAS 7 permits to be reported on a net basis, as discussed in section 5.2 below. [IAS 7.21]. 4.3 Cash flows from financing activities Financing activities are defined as those “activities that result in changes in the size and composition of the contributed equity and borrowings of the entity”. [IAS 7.6]. The standard states that this information is useful in predicting claims on future cash flows by providers of capital to the entity. [IAS 7.17]. However, it would seem more likely that information on financing cash flows would indicate the extent to which the entity has had recourse to external financing to meet its operating and investing needs in the period. The disclosure of the value and maturity of the entity’s financial liabilities, as required by IFRS 7, would contribute more to predicting future claims on cash flows. [IFRS 7.39]. Cash flows arising from financing activities include: (a) Proceeds from issuing shares or other equity instruments (b) Payments to owners to acquire or redeem the entity’s shares (c) Proceeds from issuing, and outflows to repay, debentures, loans, notes, bonds, mortgages and other short or long-term borrowings And (d) Payments by a lessee for the reduction of the outstanding liability relating to a lease [IAS 7.17] In consolidated financial statements, financing cash flows will include those arising from changes in ownership interests in a subsidiary that do not result in a loss of control (see section 9.2 below). [IAS 7.42A]. Major classes of gross receipts and gross payments arising from financing activities must be reported separately, except for those items that can be reported on a net basis, as discussed in section 5.2 below. **[IAS 7.21].** Disclosure requirements in respect of changes in liabilities arising from financing activities are discussed in section 11.2 below. 4.4 Allocating items to operating, investing and financing activities Sometimes it is not clear how to classify cash flows amongst operating, investing and financing activities. IAS 7 provides additional guidance on the classification of certain transactions, including interest, dividends and income taxes, while other questions are not addressed explicitly in the standard. These, as well as some common areas where judgement may be required to classify cash flows, are discussed below. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Statement of cash flows Where the appropriate classification is not clear from IAS 7 and an entity has therefore applied judgement to determine the appropriate presentation of material items based on the principles of IAS 7, additional disclosure may be needed to explain these judgements. IAS 1 requires disclosure of the judgements that management has made that have the most significant effect on the amounts recognised in the financial statements, [IAS 1.122], as well as information that is relevant to an understanding of any of the entity’s financial statements. [IAS 1.112]. (See Chapter 3 at section 5.1). 4.4.1 Interest and dividends An entity is required to disclose separately cash flows from interest and dividends received and paid, and their classification as either operating, investing or financing activities must be applied in a consistent manner from period to period. [IAS 7.31]. For a financial institution, interest paid, and interest and dividends received are usually classified as operating cash flows. However, IAS 7 notes that there is no consensus on the classification of these cash flows for other entities and goes on to state that: • Interest paid may be classified under either operating or financing activities. • Interest received and dividends received may be included in either operating or investing cash flows. **[IAS 7.33].** The standard allows dividends paid to be classified as a financing cash flow (because they are a cost of obtaining financial resources) or as a component of cash flows from operating activities. [IAS 7.34]. In Practical example 4-1 in section 4 above, AstraZeneca PLC has included interest paid under operating activities, interest received under investing activities and dividends paid under financing activities, as permitted by the standard. A different treatment is adopted by Telefonica, S.A. in Practical example 4-2 in section 4.1.1 above, where interest paid, interest received and dividends received are all included as operating cash flows and dividends paid are included as financing cash flows. All these treatments comply with the standard; however, the most appropriate treatment will depend on the nature of the entity and its activities. For example, if an entity does not include interest received or dividends received within revenue it may be more appropriate to include the interest or dividend cash inflows as investing cash flows rather than operating cash flows. This is because cash flows from operating activities are primarily derived from the principal revenue-generating activities of the entity, [IAS 7.14], and the amount of cash flows arising from operating activities is intended to be a key indicator of the extent to which the operations of the entity have generated sufficient cash flows to repay loans, pay dividends and make new investments without recourse to external sources of financing. [IAS 7.13]. Similarly, an entity could treat interest and dividends paid as financing cash flows because they are a cost of obtaining financial resources. [IAS 7.34]. In addition, the standard requires the total amount of interest paid during the period to be disclosed in the statement of cash flows, whether it has been recognised as an expense or capitalised as part of the cost of an asset in accordance with IAS 23 Borrowing Costs. [IAS 7.32]. A literal reading of this requirement might suggest that interest paid must be presented as a single figure under operating or financing activities. However, it could also be appropriate to classify payments of interests in accordance with the classification of the underlying asset to which those payments were capitalised provided that, when this is done, the total amount of interest paid is also disclosed either on the face of the statement of cash flows or in the notes. For example, payments of interest that is capitalised as part of the cost of property, plant and equipment would be classified as part of an entity’s investing activities, and payments of interest that is capitalised as part of the cost of inventories would be classified as part of an entity’s operating activities. An attempt to clarify the issue through an annual improvement12 was never finalised, mainly because the IFRS IC observed that amendments to IAS 7 relating to classification of cash flows should not be made on a piecemeal basis unless the classification was evident from the current guidance in IAS 7 and an amendment to IAS 7 would make that classification clearer.13 Where an entity has issued shares that are classified as financial liabilities in accordance with IAS 32 Financial Instruments: Presentation (see Chapter 43), the dividends paid on those shares will be presented in the statement of cash flows as either operating or financing in line with the entity’s presentation of other interest paid. Exposure Draft ED 2012/1, Annual Improvements to IFRSs 2010-2012 Cycle, IASB, May 2012, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013.* Statement of cash flows 4.4.2 Taxes on income Cash flows arising from taxes on income shall be separately disclosed within operating cash flows unless they can be specifically identified with investing or financing activities. [IAS 7.35]. Whilst it is possible to match elements of tax expense to transactions for which the cash flows are classified under investing or financing activities; taxes paid are usually classified as cash flows from operating activities because it is often impracticable to match tax cash flows with specific elements of tax expense. Also, those tax cash flows may arise in a different period from the underlying transaction. [IAS 7.36]. However, when it is practicable to make this determination, the tax cash flow is identified as an investing or financing activity in accordance with the individual transaction that gives rise to such cash flows. In cases where tax cash flows are allocated over more than one class of activity, disclosure is required of the total amount for taxes paid. [IAS 7.36]. 4.4.3 Sales taxes and other non-income tax cash flows Although it sets out requirements for the treatment of taxes on income, IAS 7 does not specifically address the treatment of cash flows relating to other taxes, such as value added tax (VAT) or other sales taxes and duty. The IFRS IC has considered whether it should add the question about VAT to its agenda and decided that it was not appropriate to develop an interpretation.
