In explaining why, it would not add this question to its agenda, the IFRS IC noted that “IAS 7 does not explicitly address the treatment of VAT” and added that “while different practices may emerge, they are not expected to be widespread”.14 Given the availability of alternative treatments, the IFRS IC noted that it would be appropriate in complying with IAS 1 for entities to disclose whether cash flows are presented inclusive or exclusive of related VAT.15 How we see it In our view, entities can choose to disclose sales taxes and other non-income tax receipts and payments separately in the statement of cash flows or as part of the related cash inflows and outflows. Even if presented separately, the tax cash flows are presented as operating, investing or financing in accordance with the classification of the underlying transactions that gives rise to the tax cash flows. 4.4.4 Cash contributions to a long-term employee benefit fund As discussed in section 4.1 above, the definition of operating activities in IAS 7 includes as one of its examples “cash payments to and on behalf of employees”. [IAS 7.14(d)]. Such cash outflows would typically involve wages and salaries paid to employees, taxes paid on behalf of employees and any other employee benefit contributions made on behalf of employees. Where a company makes contributions to a long-term employee benefit fund on behalf of its employees, those cash outflows are part of the compensation for employment services and are classified as operating cash flows. 4.4.5 Cash flows from factoring of trade receivables Another question not explicitly addressed in the standard is the classification of cash receipts from the factoring of trade receivables. In the case of debt factoring, an entity aims to receive cash flows from trade receivables more quickly than would arise from normal collection from customers, generally by transferring rights over those receivables to a financial institution. This raises questions as to whether the transfer results in derecognition of the trade receivables, or to the continued recognition of the trade receivables and the recognition of a financial liability for the funding received from the factoring entity. The characteristics determining which of these accounting treatments would be appropriate are discussed in Chapter 48 at sections 4.5 and 5. IFRIC Update, August 2005, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, August 2005, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows How we see it In our view, the classification of the cash receipt from the financial institution depends on the accounting treatment of the trade receivables. Only to the extent that the factoring arrangement results in the derecognition of the original trade receivable would it be appropriate to regard the cash receipt in the same way as any other receipt from the sale of goods and rendering of services and classify it in operating activities. In cases where the trade receivable is not derecognised and a liability is recorded, the nature of the arrangement is a borrowing secured against trade receivables and accordingly we believe that the cash receipt from factoring must be treated in the same way as any short-term borrowing and included in financing activities. [IAS 7.17(c)]. The later cash inflow from the customer for settlement of the trade receivable would be included in operating cash flows and the reduction in the liability to the financial institution would be a financing outflow. Following the principle in IFRS 9 for the disclosure of income and expense relating to a transferred asset that continues to be recognised, [IFRS 9.3.2.22], these two amounts would not be offset in the statement of cash flows. However, it would be acceptable for the entity to disclose the net borrowing receipts from, and repayments to, the financial institution, if it was determined that these relate to advances made for and the repayment of short-term borrowings such as those which have a maturity period of three months or less. [IAS 7.23A(c)]. In some cases, the factoring arrangement requires customers to remit cash directly to the financial institution, but the arrangement does not result in the derecognition of the original trade receivables. As, discussed above, the initial cash receipt from factoring is included in financing activities. [IAS 7.17]. However, there is currently diversity as to whether, and how, the cash received by the financial institution from customers are presented in the entity’s statement of cash flows. When the transfer does not give rise to derecognition of the trade receivable by the reporting entity, the reporting entity may determine that the factoring entity is acting as its agent and is therefore collecting the cash on its behalf before drawing down amounts to settle the liability it is owed by the reporting entity. In this case, the entity would report an operating cash inflow from the customer and a financing cash outflow to the financial institution when the financial institution collects the cash from the customers. See section 5.3 below for further discussion of the presentation of cash paid or received on an entity’s behalf. Alternatively, if the reporting entity determines that the factoring agent is not acting as its agent, the entity will reflect the later settlement of the debt by the customer as a non-cash transaction with no cash flows reported at the time of the ultimate derecognition of the trade receivable and the related factoring liability. 4.4.6 Cash flows from supply-chain financing and reverse factoring arrangements The IFRS IC discussed the application of IFRS accounting standards to reverse factoring arrangements in their June 2020 and December 2020 meetings. The IFRS IC described reverse factoring arrangements simply as ones in which a financial institution agrees to pay amounts an entity owes to the entity’s suppliers and the entity agrees to pay the financial institution at the same date as, or a date later than, suppliers are paid. An agenda decision 16 was published in December 2020, outlining how IFRS accounting standards are applied to the different aspects of accounting for such arrangements. The agenda decision considers the presentation in the statement of financial position, the derecognition of a financial liability, the presentation in the statement of cash flows and disclosure in the notes to the financial statements. Details of the agenda decision and the impact on the statement of financial position can be found in Chapter 50 at section 7.4.3.B and on the notes to the financial statements in Chapter 50 at section 7.4.3.E. In respect of the presentation in the statement of cash flows, an entity that has entered into a reverse factoring arrangement would need to determine whether to classify cash flows under the arrangement as cash flows from operating activities or cash flows from financing activities. IFRIC Update, December 2020, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows This in turn poses the following challenges: • Does the presentation of the liability to the financial institution in the statement of financial position impact the presentation of cash flows? For example, if the liability is presented outside of trade and other payables, must the ultimate payment to the financial institution be presented as a financing outflow? Or can it instead be presented as an operating cash flow based on the nature of the activities that initially led to the recognition of the trade payable that was settled through the supply-chain financing? • Will the remittance of cash directly to the supplier by the financial institution be reflected at all in the statement of cash flow or is it a non-cash transaction? With respect to the first question above, the agenda decision notes that “the committee observed that an entity’s assessment of the nature of the liabilities that are part of the arrangement may help in determining whether the related cash flows arise from operating or financing activities. For example, if the entity considers the related liability to be a trade or other payable that is part of the working capital used in the entity’s principal revenue-producing activities, the entity presents cash outflows to settle the liability as arising from operating activities in its statement of cash flows. In contrast, if the entity considers that the related liability is not a trade or other payable because the liability represents borrowings of the entity, the entity presents cash outflows to settle the liability as arising from financing activities in its statement of cash flows”. How we see it In our view, the agenda decision does not preclude an entity from presenting the repayment of the liability owed to the factor as an operating cashflow based on the nature of the transaction that led to the initial recognition of the liability to the entity’s supplier, notwithstanding a presentation in the statement of financial position of the liability to the financial institution outside of trade and other payables. This is because there is no automatic link between the statement of financial position and the statement of cash flows. ## In both cases, such presentations must be applied consistently and disclosed and explained in the notes. As regards the identification of cash flows and the second question above, the agenda decision notes that “Investing and financing transactions that do not require the use of cash or cash equivalents are excluded from an entity’s statement of cash flows (paragraph 43 of IAS 7). Consequently, if a cash inflow and cash outflow occur for an entity when an invoice is factored as part of a reverse factoring arrangement, the entity presents those cash flows in its statement of cash flows. If no cash inflow or cash outflow occurs for an entity in a financing transaction, the entity discloses the transaction elsewhere in the financial statements in a way that provides all the relevant information about the financing activity (paragraph 43 of IAS 7)”. [IAS 7.43]. How we see it In our view, the agenda decision does not go beyond confirming the requirements of the standard when it states that if no cash flows are involved, then no cash flows are presented in the cash flow statement. In practice, a careful analysis of the relationship between the entity and the financial institution is needed to determine whether the financial institution is acting as an agent on behalf of the entity and receiving and incurring cash flows on its behalf. This will inform the appropriate presentation in the statement of cash flows. We discuss this issue further, including factors to consider in analysing the relationship between the entity and the financial institution, in section 5.3 below. In its agenda decision, the IFRS IC rightly observed that assessing how to present liabilities and cash flows related to reverse factoring arrangements may involve judgement and emphasised the requirement in IAS 1 to disclose judgements that management has made in this respect if they are among the judgements made that have the most significant effect on the amounts recognised in the financial statements, [IAS 1.122], as well as any information that is relevant to an understanding of any of the entity’s financial statements. [IAS 1.112]. The agenda decision suggests that the issue is only about whether to classify the associated cash flows as operating or financing activities. However, we note that if the cost of the goods or services is capitalised into a longer-term asset such as property, plant and equipment, or possibly an intangible asset, the classification decision would be between investing and financing activities. This may be particularly relevant in asset intensive industries such as telecommunications or the extractive industries. *Reference: IASB Staff Paper, Project: Supply Chain Financing Arrangements-Reverse Factoring, Paper topic: Comment letters on tentative* agenda decision, IFRS IC Meeting, December 2020, paragraph 50. Available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows In the agenda decision the IFRS IC noted that the effects of reverse factoring arrangements may also need to be included in the disclosure of: • Changes in liabilities arising from financing activities, as required by IAS 7.44A (see section 11.2 below). If an entity classifies the cash flows arising from a reverse factoring arrangement as financing, or will do so in the future, the related liabilities and cash and non-cash changes are included in this disclosure. And • Liquidity risk, as required by IFRS 7, [IFRS 7.33, IFRS 7.34, IFRS 7.35, IFRS 7.39, IFRS 7.B11F], because the entity has concentrated a portion of its liabilities with one financial institution rather than a diverse group of suppliers - see Chapter 50 at section 7.4.3.E. Also, some suppliers may have become accustomed to, or reliant on, earlier payment of their trade receivables under the reverse factoring arrangement. If the financial institution were to withdraw the reverse factoring arrangement, those suppliers could demand shorter credit terms, which would affect the entity’s liquidity, particularly if the entity was already in financial distress. In the process of finalising the agenda decision, the IFRS IC and the Board acknowledged that user information needs may not be met by the current requirements in respect of such arrangements. This resulted in the Board issuing Supplier Finance Arrangements (Amendments to IAS 7 and IFRS 7) (the amendments) in May 2023. The disclosure requirements in the amendments are intended to assist users of financial statements in understanding the effects of supplier finance arrangements on an entity’s liabilities, cash flows and exposure to liquidity risk. The amendments, which are effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2024, are discussed in more detail in section 11.4 below. Early adoption of the amendments is permitted. However, many entities may choose to wait until the mandatory adoption date, not least because they may need to improve their financial reporting systems and/or obtain legal permission from finance providers in order to collect the information that is required to provide the new disclosures. In periods prior to the adoption of the amendments, if the entity has entered into reverse factoring arrangements, management must exercise judgement as to the appropriate level of disclosures. How we see it In our view, prior to the adoption of the amendments to IAS 7 and IFRS 7 on disclosures relating to supplier finance arrangements, additional disclosure will still be necessary whenever these arrangements have a material impact on the financial statements to explain the nature of the arrangements and amounts involved, the financial reporting judgements made and impacts thereof and, more specifically, the level of debt and the statement of cash flows. In Practical example 4-4 below, Wm Morrison Supermarkets Limited discloses how they present liabilities and cash flows associated with their supply chain financing arrangements: Practical example 4-4: Wm Morrison Supermarkets Limited (2023) United Kingdom Annual Report and Financial Statements for the 52 weeks ended 29 October 2023 [extract] Financial statements [extract] Notes to the Group financial statements [extract] ## Working capital and provisions [extract] ### Accounting policies [extract] Statement of cash flows 4.4.7 Property, plant and equipment held for rental Payments to acquire and receipts from the sale of, property, plant and equipment are usually included in investing cash flows; however, this is not always the case. A number of entities routinely sell assets that were previously held for rental, for example, car rental companies that acquire vehicles with the intention of holding them as rental cars for a limited period and then selling them. IAS 16 Property, Plant and Equipment requires an entity, that, in its ordinary course of business, routinely sells items of property, plant and equipment that it has held for rental to others, to classify the proceeds on the sale of such property, plant and equipment as revenue. [IAS 16.68A]. Accordingly, the proceeds from the sale of such assets are classified as cash flows from operating activities, as are cash payments to manufacture or acquire property, plant and equipment held for rental to others and routinely sold in the ordinary course of business. [IAS 7.14]. In our view, the requirement to classify payments for such property, plant and equipment held for rental under operating cash flows is intended to avoid initial expenditure on purchases of assets being classified as investing activities, while inflows from sales are recorded within operating activities. However, this means that management will need to determine, at the time of acquisition or manufacture, which of the assets that it intends to rent out will be ultimately held for sale in the ordinary course of business. 4.4.8 Cash received as compensation for an insured loss As noted in section 4.2 above, cash flows arising from investing activities include receipts from the sale of property, plant and equipment and other long-term assets. [IAS 7.16(b)]. This classification would also be appropriate if the receipt of cash came from insurance proceeds received to cover for the loss or damage of such assets. It would not be appropriate to present the cash inflow as an operating cash flow as the insurance proceeds are not derived from the principal revenue-generating activities of the business. [IAS 7.14]. However, the nature of the claim giving rise to the cash inflow must be carefully considered. If the claim relates only partially to the loss of damage of a long-term asset, it may be necessary for the cash inflows to be allocated between cash flows from operating activities (e.g., for the compensation for business interruption) and cash flows from investing activities. 4.4.9 Cash flows for service concession arrangements Because a cash flow is only classified in investing activities if it results in a recognised asset in the statement of financial position, a question arises regarding the classification of the cash inflows and outflows of the operator of a service concession arrangement that is within the scope of IFRIC 12 Service Concession Arrangements. IFRIC 12 features two possible accounting models - the intangible asset model or the financial asset model. Under both models, the service element relating to the construction of the infrastructure asset is accounted for in accordance with IFRS 15 and the revenue recognised gives rise to an intangible or a financial asset, in the form of a receivable, respectively. It is unclear whether cash flows incurred in the construction or upgrade phase must always be regarded as operating cash flows because they relate to the provision of construction services; or whether they are more accurately classified as investing activities, as they reflect cash outflows that result in the recognition of an asset. In the case of an arrangement under the intangible asset model, the cash flows incurred during construction or upgrading could be classified in investing activities as they relate to the acquisition of an intangible that will generate future income and cash flows. Once the operating phase is reached, the cash inflows received would be most appropriately classified in operating activities as most operating and maintenance costs are likely to be executory and will be accounted for as incurred. On the other hand, when the financial asset model applies, cash outflows during the construction phase may be considered to represent the provision of financing to the grantor and as such an investing outflow. In a corollary of the discussion on deferred payments in section 7.1 below, where the time value of money is significant to the transaction, the substance of the transaction may be the provision of a loan. Where this is the case the repayment of such an instrument by the grantor during the operating phase would be considered an investing cash flow (or potentially split between investing and operating cash flows for the capital and interest components respectively, depending on the policy of the entity). Since there is no specific guidance relating to the classification of cash flows for service concession arrangements, current practice is mixed. IFRIC 12 is discussed in more detail in Chapter 26. Statement of cash flows 4.4.10 Treasury shares Treasury shares are an entity’s own equity instruments that are acquired and held by the entity, a subsidiary, or other members of the consolidated group. The consideration paid or received for treasury shares is recognised directly in equity and not as a movement in investments. [IAS 32.33]. Similarly, IAS 7 requires that payments and receipts to acquire or dispose of treasury shares are classified within financing activities. [IAS 7.17]. Even where such treasury shares are acquired by the entity as part of an equity-settled share-based payment transaction, the cash outflow is classified under financing activities. Whilst cash payments to and on behalf of employees are classified under operating activities, [IAS 7.14], the acquisition of treasury shares does not settle a transaction between the entity and its employees. An equity-settled share-based payment transaction is completed when the entity transfers its equity instruments to employees in consideration for the services received. When a cash payment is made by a subsidiary to its parent or a trust that holds treasury shares as part of an equity-settled share-based payment arrangement, the payment is accounted for as a deduction from equity in the separate financial statements of the subsidiary, on the grounds that the payment does not settle the transaction with the employees, but is effectively a distribution to the parent or the trust (see # Chapter 30 at section 12.2.7). Having regarded this as a distribution, it follows that the cash flow must be classified as either operating or financing, according to the entity’s policy on dividends as discussed in section 4.4.1 above. 4.4.11 Cash flows related to the costs of a share issue Costs directly related to the issue of shares are required to be deducted from equity. [IAS 32.35]. As the costs reduce the amount of the proceeds received from the share issue, they are classified as a financing cash flow. [IAS 7.17(a)]. However, where a proposed share issue is cancelled, there would be no proceeds from the issue to record and the related expenses would be included in profit and loss rather than equity. As such, the definition of a financing cash flow would not be met, and the transaction costs would be classified in operating cash flows. 4.4.12 Cash flows paid or received under derivative contracts Payments and receipts relating to derivative contracts can be classified within operating, investing or financing in different circumstances. Where the contract is held for dealing or trading purposes, the cash flows are classified under operating activities. [IAS 7.14(g)]. IAS 7 requires that payments for, and receipts from, futures contracts, forward contracts, option contracts and swap contracts are classified as cash flows from investing activities, except when the contracts are held for dealing or trading purposes, or the cash flows are classified as financing activities. [IAS 7.16(g)]. The standard adds that when a contract is accounted for as a hedge of an identifiable position, the cash flows of the contract are classified under the same heading as the cash flows of the position being hedged. [IAS 7.16]. An example is an interest rate swap. An entity wishing to convert an existing fixed rate borrowing into a floating rate equivalent could enter into an interest rate swap under which it receives interest at fixed rates and pays at floating rates. All the cash flows under the swap are reported under the same cash flow heading as interest paid (i.e., as financing activities or operating activities, in accordance with the entity’s determined policy, as discussed in section 4.4.1 above), because they are equivalent to interest or are hedges of interest payments. More generally, IFRS 9 adds to the requirements of IAS 7 by clarifying that cash flows arising from hedging instruments are classified as operating, investing or financing activities, on the basis of the classification of the cash flows arising from the hedged item and that this should be consistent with the classification of these instruments as hedging instruments under IFRS 9. [IFRS 9.IG G.2]. This seems to leave an open question as to how an entity classifies the cash flows from a derivative contract that is considered by management as part of a hedging relationship, but for which the entity elects not to apply hedge accounting (taking all movements to profit or loss)? Since IAS 7 has not been updated to reflect IFRS 9, this question is not dealt with explicitly in IAS 7. How we see it In our view, if a hedging relationship would qualify for hedge accounting had it been so designated, but has not been accounted for applying the hedge accounting requirements of IFRS 9, it would be permitted for the cash flows from the hedging instrument to be presented in the same category as the cash flows of the position being hedged. Statement of cash flows This can be explained in the following Illustration: Illustration 4-1: Cash flows from derivatives not designated as hedges Company A has the euro as its functional currency. On 1 January 20X1, it sells goods to a US customer for which it charges US$1,000,000. The spot exchange rate on this date is 1:1 and it recognises revenue of €1,000,000. Payment is due to be received on 30 June 20X1. A enters into a forward contract to exchange US$1,000,000 for €1,095,000 on 30 June 20X1. The hedging relationship would qualify to be accounted for applying the hedge accounting requirements of IFRS 9. However, Company A does not designate it as a hedge because the effects of movements on the contract and those of retranslating the receivable will already offset in profit or loss. On 30 June 20X1 the exchange rate is such that A receives the equivalent of €1,200,000 from its customer and pays €105,000 on the forward contract. Taken literally, IAS 7 would suggest that, because the forward contract is not held for dealing or trading purposes and is not accounted for as a hedge of an identifiable position, the €105,000 cash outflow on the forward contract cannot be classified under operating activities. As such, the €105,000 would have to appear in investing or possibly financing cash flows. However, had the entity elected to apply hedge accounting, the standard would require the €105,000 to be included in operating cash flows. Therefore, as explained above, in our view entity A could include the payment on the forward contract in cash flows from operating activities. 4.4.13 Debt instrument issued at a discount or redeemed at a premium Cash outflows that represent the repayment of the principal amount of a loan or other debt instrument are classified as financing activities. [IAS 7.17]. As noted in section 4.4.1 above, interest paid may be classified under either operating or financing activities. Therefore, where an entity adopts a policy of classifying interest under operating activities, it may be necessary to consider carefully the nature of any payment to the lender to ensure the appropriate classification between operating and financing. This will be particularly relevant where the pattern of payments differs from the recognition of the interest expense in profit or loss. For example, a company that issues a zero-coupon bond at nominal value, with a premium payable on redemption, will not make any interest payments over the life of the bond. However, in accounting for the financial liability at amortised cost under IFRS 9, the entity will recognise interest at the EIR, calculated to reflect the premium payable on redemption, as explained in Chapter 46 at section 3.1. This raises the question of how to present the cash paid on redemption. Is it appropriate to present the entire cash outflow within financing activities as the redemption of a debt instrument? How we see it In our view, when an entity adopts a policy of classifying interest under operating activities and the cash paid on redemption is presented in the statement of cash flows, a split must be made to show the amount that relates to the interest cost (within operating activities) and the amount that relates to the repayment of the principal (within financing activities). The same principles would be applied where a debt is issued at a discount or in any other situation where the pattern of cash flows relating to interest on a financial liability are not reflected in the pattern of recognition of the interest cost. 4.4.14 Early settlement of a debt instrument A similar issue to that discussed in sections 4.4.13 above is how to present the cash payments on early settlement of a loan or other borrowing. As already noted in section 4.3 above, cash flows arising from financing activities include outflows to repay borrowings. However, in some situations an element of the cash paid on early settlement could be viewed as compensation to the lender for lost interest. Lenders will often include a prepayment penalty provision in loan agreements that can be based on a number of factors, including an approximation of the interest that will not be paid as a result of early settlement. This would support a split presentation of the cash outflow between repayment of borrowings and cash outflow for interest. Alternatively, the nature of the entire cash outflow may also be viewed as relating to a financing activity, being the extinguishment of a liability, which is then be presented as one amount in the financing activities section of the statement. 4.4.15 Receipt of a government grant The accounting requirements for government grants are set out in IAS 20 Accounting for Government Grants and Disclosure of Government Assistance and are covered in Chapter 25. Statement of cash flows Neither IAS 20 nor IAS 7 provide guidance on where in the statement of cash flows to present the cash inflows from the receipt of government grants. The IFRS IC discussed in July 2012 the presentation of cash flows for several fact patterns including government grants.18 The issue was further discussed in March 2013. The IFRS IC observed that the primary principle behind the classification of cash flows in IAS 7 is that cash flows should be classified on the basis of the nature of the activity in a manner that is most appropriate to the business of the entity in accordance with paragraph 11 of IAS 7 and with the definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in paragraph 6 of IAS 7. In the course of the discussions held, staff initially suggested that grants were providing a source of financing to the entity, and consequently cash received would be part of the entity’s financing activities. In a later meeting, however, it reached a different conclusion that cash received from a government grant should be presented as an operating cash flow instead, on the basis that although cash inflows from a grant provide the entity with financing for the designated asset/activity, a grant does not represent a borrowing activity because there is no expectation that the resource will be returned to the provider of finance; or that the provider of finance will be appropriately compensated. Eventually, the IFRS IC concluded that clarifying the application of the primary classification principle in IAS 7 was a matter too broad for it to address and discontinued its work.19 Consequently, until and unless the IASB addresses this issue as part of its upcoming project on the cash flow statement, there is likely to be diversity in practice for the presentation of all government grants in the statement of cash flows, whether related to assets or to income. Cash flows received from government grants could be considered to be financing by nature because they provide a source of financing to the entity. Alternatively, cash flows received from government grants (or only those from grants related to income) could be presented as operating cash flows by default since they do not seem to meet the definition of financing activities and cash flows received from government grants related to assets could be presented as investing cash flows as they relate to an investing activity. How we see it Selecting the most appropriate presentation will be a matter of judgement. Once adopted, the presentation principles must be applied consistently and clear disclosure of the principles and the impact thereof on the cash flow statement must be provided in accordance with IAS 1. As discussed in section 5.2.1 below, IAS 7 requires the cash inflow from the receipt of a government grant to be presented gross in the statement of cash flows as there is no exception provided to the presentation of gross cash receipts and payments in the standard for such transactions. IAS 20 further emphasises this requirement specifically for government grants related to assets regardless of whether or not the government grant is deducted from the related asset for presentation purposes in the statement of financial position. [IAS 20.28]. 4.4.16 Classification of cash flows - future impact of IFRS 18 Presentation and Disclosure in Financial statements IFRS 18 was issued in April 2024 and becomes effective for reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2027. The standard will replace IAS 1 and makes limited consequential amendments to the current requirements of IAS 7. These are explained in Chapter 4 at section 5.1. IFRIC Update, July 2012, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. IFRIC Update, March 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows Other cash flow presentation matters 5.1 Material cash flows IAS 1 requires the nature and amount of material items of income and expense to be disclosed separately. [IAS 1.97]. It also requires additional line items, headings and sub-totals to be presented on the face of the statement of financial position or of the statement(s) presenting profit or loss and other comprehensive income when this is relevant to an understanding of the entity’s financial position. [IAS 1.55, IAS 1.85]. IAS 1 does not refer to material cash flows and IAS 7 contains no specific requirements in relation to such disclosures. However, it may be similarly appropriate for material cash flows or cash flows relating to material items in the statements of financial position or of comprehensive income to be presented as separate line items on the face of the statement of cash flows, although they would remain classified according to their nature as either operating, investing or financing cash flows. [IAS 1.97]. How we see it In our view, where specific events and transactions significantly affect the amount of cash flows reported in the cash flow statement, they must be disclosed and explained in the notes in accordance with IAS 1, regardless of whether they are presented as separate line items on the face of the cash flow statement. Examples of such events and transactions are the receipt of significant government grants, a change in the pattern of payments to suppliers or from customers or in the contractual terms of payments, the introduction of trade receivables factoring arrangements or supply chain financing or extension of existing arrangements. 5.2 Gross or net presentation of cash flows Major classes of gross receipts and gross payments arising from investing and financing activities must be reported separately. [IAS 7.21]. IAS 7 permits but does not require, certain cash flows to be presented on a net basis as follows: [IAS 7.22]. Extract from IAS 7 Cash flows arising from the following operating, investing or financing activities may be reported on a net basis: (a) cash receipts and payments on behalf of customers when the cash flows reflect the activities of the customer rather than those of the entity; and (b) cash receipts and payments for items in which the turnover is quick, the amounts are large, and the maturities are short. Examples given in the standard of cash receipts and payments that reflect the activities of customers rather than those of the entity are the acceptance and repayment of demand deposits by a bank, funds held for customers by an investment company and rents collected on behalf of, and paid over to, the owners of properties. [IAS 7.23]. Examples given in the standard of cash receipts and payments in which turnover is quick, the amounts are large, and the maturities are short include advances made for and the repayment of: (a) Principal amounts relating to credit card customers (b) The purchase and sale of investments (c) Other short-term borrowings, such as those with a maturity on draw down of three months or less **[IAS 7.23A]** See section 10.2 below for discussion of the gross or net presentation of cash flows for financial institutions. Statement of cash flows 5.2.1 Gross or net presentation of cash flows - government grants IAS 7 requires the cash inflow from the receipt of a government grant to be presented gross in the statement of cash flows as there is no exception provided to the presentation of gross cash receipts and payments in the standard for such transactions. IAS 20 further emphasises this requirement specifically for grants related to assets regardless of whether or not the government grant is deducted from the related asset for presentation purposes in the statement of financial position. [IAS 20.28]. The classification of cash flows from the receipt of a government grant is discussed in section 4.4.15 above. Government grants are discussed in more detail in Chapter 25. 5.3 Presentation of cash paid or received on an entity’s behalf As discussed in section 7 below, IAS 7 requires that transactions that do not require the use of cash or cash equivalents are excluded from an entity’s statement of cash flows. [IAS 7.43]. Examples of such non-cash transactions provided in the standard are the conversion of debt to equity; acquiring assets by assuming directly related liabilities or by entering into a lease; and issuing equity as consideration for the acquisition of another entity. [IAS 7.44]. However, there is no broader guidance in IAS 7, or elsewhere in IFRS accounting standards, to help determine whether cash flows should be included in the statement of cash flows because the entity has directed another party to pay and/or receive cash on its behalf and so, in substance, the cash flows are the entity’s cash flows. This is a matter of judgement based on facts and circumstances. There may be transactions that, in substance, involve cash flows of the entity, without the cash resources of the entity actually changing. For example, where an entity purchases an item of property and finances the purchase by borrowing the funds from a financial institution, it may arrange for the money to be paid directly by the financial institution to the seller rather than receive the cash inflow from the loan into its bank account and make a subsequent cash payment to the seller. The relationship between the entity and the financial institution may be determined to be fiduciary in nature with the financial institution acting as an agent of the entity and receiving and incurring cash flows on its behalf. In all situations, the nature of the relationship with the third party will need careful examination, including assessment of the terms and conditions of each specific arrangement. Factors to consider as part of the consideration could include: • Who is directing the payment or receipt? If the reporting entity is directing the movement of the cash, it is more likely that the third party will be acting as its agent and therefore the cash flows will be cash flows of the entity. • Who controls the timing and amount of the payment or receipt? If the reporting entity has control over the timing and the amount this may suggest the third party is acting as its agent and that the payment or receipt is, in substance, the entity’s cash flow. • Why has the arrangement with the third party been established and who benefits from the arrangement? If the arrangement has been established for the convenience of the reporting entity (for example because they do not have a bank account in a certain jurisdiction) this may provide evidence that the cash flow is, in substance, the entity’s cash flow. How we see it Where it is determined that cash flows made or received by a third party are, in substance, cash flows of the entity, these cash flows must be reported in the statement of cash flows of the entity. Appropriate disclosures will be important, in particular, disclosure of the facts considered, and the judgements management has made in applying IAS 7 in accordance with paragraphs 117 and 122 of IAS 1 (see Chapter 3 at section 5.1.1.A). This issue is also discussed in the context of specific scenarios in section 4.4.5 above (factoring of trade receivables), section 4.4.6 above (supply-chain financing arrangements and reverse factoring arrangements), section 8.1 below (the in-substance purchase of an asset) and section 9.5.3 below (group treasury arrangements). Statement of cash flows Foreign currency cash flows Foreign currency cash flows can arise either when an entity enters into transactions where the cash payments or receipts are denominated in a foreign currency or when the consolidated financial statements of a group include the cash flows of a subsidiary whose functional currency is different from the presentation currency of the group (e.g., a foreign subsidiary). IAS 21 The Effects of Changes in Foreign Exchange Rates excludes from its scope the translation of cash flows of a foreign operation and the presentation of foreign currency cash flows in a statement of cash flows. [IAS 21.7]. Nevertheless, IAS 7 requires foreign currency cash flows to be reported in a manner consistent with IAS 21. [IAS 7.27]. The requirements for entities falling within the scope of IAS 29 Financial Reporting in Hyperinflationary Economies are discussed in Chapter 17. 6.1 Foreign currency denominated transactions When an entity enters into a transaction denominated in a currency other than its functional currency, there are no direct consequences for the statement of cash flows until payments are received or made. Once payment is made or received, the cash outflows or inflows are recorded in the entity’s accounting records at the exchange rate prevailing at the date of payment and these amounts are reflected in the statement of cash flows. [IAS 7.25]. If the entity’s presentation currency is different from its functional currency, the functional currency cash flows are translated into the presentation currency at the rates at the date of the cash flows. [IAS 7.26]. For practical reasons, an entity can apply a rate that approximates the actual rate on the date of the cash flow (such as a weighted average for a period) but, like the requirements of IAS 21 for income and expenses, translation using the exchange rate as at the end of the reporting period is not permitted. [IAS 7.27]. For operating cash flows presented using the direct method (see section 4.1.1 above), investing and financing cash flows, these requirements are relatively easy to understand, i.e., the amounts reported should be the cash paid or received, translated at the exchange rate prevailing at the date of the payment or receipt or a rate approximating that. For operating cash flows presented using the indirect method, there are additional complications which are considered below. 6.1.1 Operating cash flows presented using the indirect method For operating cash flows presented using the indirect method, the entity presents the net cash flows from operating activities by working back from amounts reported in the income statement (see section 4.1.2 above). The most common approach adjusts reported profit or loss for the effects of: (a) Changes in inventories and operating receivables and payables during the period (b) Non-cash items such as depreciation, provisions, deferred taxes, unrealised foreign currency gains and losses, and undistributed profits of associates And (c) All other items for which the cash effects are investing or financing cash flows [IAS 7.20] The exchange differences reported in profit or loss may include realised and unrealised gains and losses arising from operating activities and investing and financing activities. Entities must therefore determine what adjustments to make to profit or loss to ensure that foreign currency items are only included in the operating cash flow reconciliation to the extent that cash flows have occurred and that they relate to operating activities. This is discussed in the sections below. 6.1.1.A Realised and unrealised gains and losses relating to operating activities For realised gains or losses that relate to operating items such as sales or purchases of inventory by an entity, no adjustment is needed to get from profit or loss to operating cash flows if the starting point for the reconciliation is profit before tax or profit after tax. This is because it will include both the amount recorded at the date of sale and the amount of the exchange difference on settlement, the combination of which gives the amount of the actual cash flow. For unrealised gains or losses that relate to operating items, no adjustment will be needed as the unrealised exchange gain or loss is generally already reflected in the movement in the related payable or receivable included in the reconciliation to operating cash flows. Alternatively, some entities may choose to reflect these gains and losses as a reconciling item and then also include an equivalent adjustment in the movement in the related receivable or payable, however this method is rarely seen in practice. Statement of cash flows 6.1.1.B Realised and unrealised gains and losses relating to non-operating activities For realised gains or losses arising on a settled transaction relating to investing or financing activities, an adjustment is needed to reconcile reported profit or loss to the net cash flow from operating activities. For example, the foreign currency purchase of property, plant and equipment would be recorded at the rate prevailing on the date of the purchase and, if not paid for immediately, a creditor would be recorded for the same amount. Any exchange difference arising on final settlement of the creditor would be taken to profit or loss as an exchange gain or loss. If left unadjusted, operating cash flows determined using the indirect approach would include this amount of exchange gain or loss. In addition, the investing cash flow for the asset purchase would be unadjusted and recorded at the purchase date exchange rate, rather than at the exchange rate prevailing at the date of settlement of the creditor. Therefore, this difference needs to be considered in calculating the cash flow to be shown under the relevant classification, in this case investing cash flows, so that the cash flow for the purchase of the asset reflects the exchange rate at the date of settlement of the related creditor. The difference also needs to be adjusted by subtracting or adding back the exchange gain or loss in the reconciliation of operating cash flows. For unrealised gains or losses relating to investing or financing activities, an adjustment must be made to reconcile reported profit or loss to the net cash flow from operating activities. This is because the unrealised exchange differences will be reflected in the income statement as an exchange gain or loss. If left unadjusted, the reconciliation of operating cash flows prepared using the indirect approach would total to a figure for operating cash flows which would incorrectly include this amount of non-operating, unrealised exchange gain or loss. 6.2 Cash and cash equivalent balances denominated in foreign currencies Unrealised gains and losses arising from exchange rate movements on foreign currency cash and cash equivalents are not cash flows. However, it is necessary to include these exchange differences in the statement of cash flows in order to reconcile the movement in cash and cash equivalents at the beginning and end of the reporting period. The effect of exchange rate movements on cash and cash equivalents is presented as a single amount at the foot of the statement of cash flows, separately from operating, investing and financing cash flows and includes the differences, if any, had those cash flows been reported at end of period exchange rates. [IAS 7.28]. An example of this is illustrated in Practical example 4-1 in section 4 above. This reconciling item reflects: • The difference between the opening and closing exchange rate used multiplied by the foreign currency cash balances held at the beginning of the year. And • The difference between the exchange rates used to translate foreign currency cash flows (e.g., an average rate) and the closing exchange rate multiplied by the net cash flow activity for the period measured in the foreign currency. 6.3 Consolidated financial statements including foreign subsidiaries Section 9.1 below discusses the general principles of preparing a consolidated statement of cash flows. When the group contains a foreign subsidiary, the cash flows of that subsidiary must be translated into the presentation currency of the group using the exchange rates prevailing at the dates of the cash flows. [IAS 7.26]. This is similar to the treatment of foreign currency transactions discussed at section 6.1 above. A rate that approximates the actual rate on the date of the cash flow (such as a weighted average for a period) can be used. [IAS 7.27]. However, this does not apply to the cash flows of a foreign subsidiary whose functional currency is the currency of a hyperinflationary economy as all amounts, including cash flows, are translated at the closing rate at the date of the most recent statement of financial position. See Chapter 16 at section 6.1 for more discussion on translating amounts in a functional currency that is the currency of a hyperinflationary economy. Statement of cash flows 6.3.1 The indirect method and foreign subsidiaries Entities should exercise care when applying the indirect method at the ‘more consolidated level’ as described in section 9.1 below when there are foreign subsidiaries. If the translated financial statements are used, exchange differences will be included in the movements between the opening and closing group balance sheets. For example, if inventories held by a US subsidiary of a UK parent increase from $240 to $270 during the year and the opening exchange rate of £1=$1.60 becomes £1=$1.80 by the year-end, inventories will be reported as an unchanged amount of £150 in both balance sheets. This could result in the reconciliation showing no movement between opening and closing inventories which would be incorrect. In these circumstances it would be advisable for an entity to take the functional currency financial statements of the foreign subsidiary as the starting point, or otherwise ensure the exchange differences have been correctly reflected. 6.3.2 Exchange differences on retranslation of foreign subsidiaries reported in other comprehensive income. All exchange differences arising on translation of foreign operations will have been recognised in other comprehensive income - see Chapter 16 at section 6.1. Such exchange differences are not cash flows, and will therefore not be reported as such in the consolidated statement of cash flows. However, any exchange differences relating to the translation of cash and cash equivalents held in foreign currencies by the foreign subsidiaries will be included in the reconciliation of cash and cash equivalents at the beginning and end of the period, as reported at the foot of the statements of cash flows - see section 6.2 above. Non-cash transactions and transactions on deferred terms Non-cash transactions only ever appear in a statement of cash flows as adjustments to profit or loss for the period when using the indirect method of presenting cash flows from operating activities as discussed in section 4.1.2 above. Investing and financing transactions that do not involve cash or cash equivalents are always excluded from the statement of cash flows. Disclosure is required elsewhere in the financial statements in order to provide all relevant information about these investing and financing activities. [IAS 7.43]. Examples of such non-cash transactions include the conversion of debt to equity; acquiring assets by assuming directly related liabilities or by entering into a lease; and issuing equity as consideration for the acquisition of another entity. [IAS 7.44]. Similarly, asset exchange transactions and the issue of bonus shares out of retained earnings are disclosed as non-cash transactions. Practical example 7-1 below shows the disclosures made by China Mobile Limited. Practical example 7-1: China Mobile Limited (2020) Hong Kong ANNUAL REPORT 2020 [extract] Consolidated Statement of Cash Flows [extract] … … 7.1 Asset purchased on deferred terms from the supplier When an asset is purchased on deferred terms granted by the supplier directly, it may not be clear whether the associated cash flows should be classified within investing activities or within financing activities, as the repayment of borrowings. Statement of cash flows It could be argued that the payment of a deferred amount would not meet the definition of investing cash flows, because it does not result in recognition of an asset, but rather a reduction of a liability. However, as discussed in section 4 above, the IFRS IC and the IASB have affirmed in 2013 their position that in determining the classification of cash flows, the nature of the activity is still the primary principle to be considered.20 Accordingly, the classification of the payment comes down to a judgement as to whether its nature relates to the acquisition of an asset or the repayment of a liability. How we see it The settlement of a short-term payable for the purchase of an asset is an investing cash flow. Where the period between acquisition and payment is significant, judgement must be applied to determine whether the definition of a financing cash flow has now been met. One method that may be used to determine whether the transaction involves a financing component is to look to the requirements in IFRS 15 in respect of whether a contract contains a significant financing component (see Chapter 28 at section 5.5). [IFRS 15.60]. The liability recorded for the deferred consideration would be classified and subsequently measured in accordance with IFRS 9 (see Chapter 44 at section 2). In this case, the liability might need to be split to separate cash flows for the interest components, depending on the accounting policy of the entity - see section 4.4.1 above. Where an asset is acquired on deferred terms it would, where significant, require disclosure as a non-cash transaction in accordance with paragraph 43 of IAS 7 (see section 11.3 below). Where an entity acquires a right-of-use asset under a lease, IAS 7 is clear that the transaction is a non-cash transaction. [IAS 7.44]. The cash flow presentation of leasing transactions is discussed in section 8 below. 7.2 Asset disposals on deferred terms As explained in section 7 above, investing and financing transactions that do not involve cash or cash equivalents are always excluded from the statement of cash flows and disclosure is required of these non- cash transactions. [IAS 7.43]. Therefore, if an asset is sold but no payments is received in the period of the sale, there will be no amounts reported in the statement of cash flows. Where the arrangement is determined to be the provision of finance by the vendor, for example the derecognition of property, plant and equipment by the lessor under a finance lease, subsequent cash receipts to reduce the receivable from the purchaser would be investing cash flows, but appropriately described, for example as the receipt of deferred consideration rather than the proceeds on sale of property, plant and equipment. [IAS 7.16]. 7.3 Revenue contracts with deferred payment terms As discussed in Chapter 28 at section 5.5, a contract with a customer that has a significant financing component would be separated into a revenue component (for the notional cash sales price) and a loan component (for the effect of the deferred or advance payment terms). [IFRS 15.60]. In the case of deferred payment terms this gives rise to two cash flow components i.e., the revenue component cash flows must be classified as cash flows from operating activities, and the cash flows related to the significant financing component must be classified consistently with the entity’s choice to present cash flows from interest received. [IAS 7.31, IAS 7.33]. If the customer pays in advance, however, the sum of the cash amount and the accrued interest represent revenue, and thus there is only one cash flow component (being the advance payment) which must be classified as a cash inflow from operating activities. [IAS 7.14]. *Reference: IASB Update, April 2013, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website.* Statement of cash flows 7.4 Variable and contingent consideration for asset acquisitions outside of a business combination Contingent consideration paid on a business combination is discussed in section 9.3.3 below. The purchase price of intangible assets or tangible assets acquired outside of a business combination also sometimes includes variable or contingent consideration for example to resolve an uncertainty or a difference in views about the initial value of the asset transferred. Accounting for the contingent consideration element of such asset purchases is discussed in Chapter 18 at section 4.5 for intangible assets, and Chapter 19 at section 4.1.9 for property, plant and equipment. As explained in those chapters, such transactions can be very complex and payment can be dependent on a number of factors, with no specific guidance provided in IAS 38 Intangible Assets or IAS 16. Therefore, an entity must develop and consistently apply an accounting policy for the initial and subsequent measurement of such assets that results in information that is relevant and reliable in its particular circumstances. Once such an accounting policy is developed, the presentation in the statement of cash flows should be informed by the accounting treatment adopted in the statement of financial position. As discussed in section 7.1 above, even though the reason for the cash flows in a genuine contingent consideration is to acquire a long-term asset, classifying the resulting cash flows as investing would only be appropriate if the cash flows are in settlement of an expenditure that results in a recognised asset in the statement of financial position. [IAS 7.16]. This is because paragraph 16 of the standard requires the classification of cash flows from investing activities in the statement of cash flows to be aligned with the presentation of recognised assets in the statement of financial position rather than to reflect an entity’s judgement as to the most appropriate classification based the entity’s business and the definitions of investing activities. [IAS 7.BC5, IAS 7.BC6, IAS 7.BC7]. How we see it In our view, cash payments up to the amount capitalised in relation to the acquired assets would be classified in investing activities. Since variable and contingent consideration generally arises for reasons other than the provision of finance to the acquirer, any resulting payments would generally not seem to be eligible for classification as financing cash flows. As a result, any variable and contingent consideration in excess of the amount that has been capitalised as part of the cost of the asset (whether on initial recognition or to reflect changes in variable and contingent consideration that have been capitalised in line with the entity’s accounting policy for such payments), would need to be presented as operating cash flows by default. However, if the accounting variability of the contingent consideration is driven from factors such as currency, time value of money or the existence of an embedded derivative that are within the scope of other standards (e.g., IAS 21, IFRS 9), the cash flow presentation must be consistent with such standards. Statement of cash flows Cash flows from leasing transactions 8.1 Accounting as lessee As noted in section 7.1 above, where an entity acquires a right-of-use asset under a lease, the acquisition of the asset is clearly a non-cash transaction. The requirements of IFRS 16 are covered in Chapter 24. The presentation and disclosure requirements of IFRS 16 for lessees are discussed in Chapter 24 at sections 5.7 and 5.8. In relation to the cash flow presentation, a lessee classifies: (a) Cash payments for the principal portion of the lease liability within financing activities. (b) Cash payments for the interest portion of the lease liability in a consistent manner to the presentation other interest paid (i.e., as operating or financing - see section 4.4.1 above). And (c) Short-term lease payments, payments for leases of low-value assets and variable lease payments not included in the measurement of the lease liability within operating activities. [IFRS 16.50]. IFRS 16 also requires disclosure of the total cash outflow for leases. [IFRS 16.53(g)]. This will include lease payments for short-term leases and leases of low-value assets and variable lease payments. See Chapter 24 at section 5.8. A lessee must also include lease liabilities (and the related cash and non-cash movements) in the disclosures required by IAS 7.44A of changes in liabilities arising from financing activities - see section 11.2 below. However, care may be needed sometimes to distinguish a lease from a purchase of an asset based on the substance of that transaction and not its legal form. IFRS 16 applies to contracts that convey the right to use an underlying asset for a period of time and does not apply to transactions that transfer control of the underlying asset to an entity - such transactions are sales or purchases within the scope of other standards. [IFRS 16.BC139, IFRS 16.BC140]. Consequently, if a contract grants rights that represent the in-substance purchase of an item of property, plant and equipment, such transaction may need to be presented as the purchase of the underlying asset (regardless of whether legal title transfers) either on deferred terms (see section 7.1 above) if entered into directly with the manufacturer or dealer of the asset or together with the provision of financing if entered into with a financial institution which purchases the underlying asset on the entity’s behalf from a designated supplier. In the latter case, this raises the question as to whether the payment of the supplier directly by the financial institution will be treated as a non-cash transaction or as a transaction in which the financial institution is acting as a paying agent for the entity. In this case, the entity would report an investing cash outflow to the supplier and a financing cash inflow from the financial institution upon taking control of the underlying asset and subsequent financing cash outflows when the liability is settled in future periods. In all cases, determining whether another party is acting as agent for the entity is key to whether the cash flows are presented in the statement of cash flows or whether the transaction is disclosed as a non-cash transaction. In all these situations, the nature of the relationship with the third party will need careful examination to ensure that the entity complies with the requirement in IAS 7 that “investing and financing transactions that do not require the use of cash or cash equivalents are excluded from an entity’s statement of cash flows”. [IAS 7.43]. Factors to consider in making this determination are discussed in section 5.3 above. 8.2 Payments made by the lessee before commencement date Where a lessee makes a lease payment to the lessor at or before the commencement date, this payment is included in the initial measurement of the right-of-use asset (see Chapter 24 at section 5.2.1). The nature of the activity giving rise to this cash outflow must be considered to determine how it is presented in the statement of cash flows. For example, if the payment represents all or substantially all of the total lease payments to be made over the lease term, the lessee classifies the cash outflow as an investing activity, being the acquisition of the right of use asset. [IAS 7.16]. In contrast, if the payment is only the first of the periodic lease payments that the lessee will make and is financing in nature, being a payment for the reduction of the lease liability, the cash outflow would then be classified as a financing activity. [IAS 7.17(e)]. Statement of cash flows 8.3 Lease incentives Lease incentives are defined in IFRS 16 as “payments made by a lessor to a lessee associated with a lease, or the reimbursement or assumption by a lessor of costs of a lessee” and are discussed in Chapter 24 at section 4.5.2. [IFRS 16 Appendix A]. Neither IFRS 16 nor IAS 7 provide specific guidance on the presentation of the cash inflows from lease incentives. It is however clear that IAS 7 considers a lease transaction to be essentially the provision of finance by the lessor to the lessee. [IAS 7.17(e)]. IFRS 16 defines lease payments as “Payments made by a lessee to a lessor relating to the right to use an underlying asset during the lease term … less any lease incentives”. [IFRS 16 Appendix A]. Therefore, lease incentives are clearly a reduction of the payments made to the lessor as repayment for the financing provided. How we see it Considering this definition of lease payments in the context of IAS 7, in our view, the appropriate presentation of cash flows from lease incentives would be to present the corresponding cash flows as part of financing activities in the statement of cash flows, regardless of whether they are received after the commencement date of the lease and are deducted from the lease liability or before the lease commencement date. Where lease incentives are received in the same period that lease payments are made, these cash inflows and outflows must, where material, be separately presented and not netted. See section 5.2 above. 8.4 Sale and leaseback transactions A sale and leaseback transaction involves the transfer of an asset by an entity (the seller-lessee) to another entity (the buyer-lessor) and the leaseback of the same asset by the seller-lessee. Sale and leaseback transactions are discussed in Chapter 24 at section 8. In terms of presentation in the statement of cash flows, IFRS 16 refers only to leases, requiring the cash payments for the principal portion of the lease liability to be presented in financing activities and cash payments for the interest portion to be presented in in a consistent manner to the presentation other interest paid - see section 8.1 above. [IFRS 16.50]. Nothing is included in IFRS 16 or IAS 7 with respect to cash flow presentation in the case of a sale and leaseback transaction. Presentation of the cash flows relating to the sale and leaseback transaction for a seller-lessee will depend on how the transaction is accounted for under IFRS 16, specifically whether the transfer of the asset is accounted for as a sale of the asset in accordance with the requirements of IFRS 15. [IFRS 16.99]. If the transaction does not satisfy the requirements of IFRS 15 to be accounted for as a sale (i.e., not a sale and leaseback transaction), the cash received is classified in the cash flow statement as a cash inflow from financing activities because the substance of the transaction is that the lessee has raised finance, with the asset provided as security. The resulting financial liability is accounted for under IFRS 9, [IFRS 16.103], and the seller-lessee splits the cash outflows between repayment of the principal and payment of interest. Repayments of the principal are classified as financing cash flows, whereas interest must be presented in a manner consistent with the presentation of other interest paid in statement of cash flows (see section 4.4.1 above). [IFRS 16.50, IFRS 16.BC211]. If the transaction does satisfy the requirements of IFRS 15 to be accounted for as a sale, the analysis is more complex, as the cash received comprises an amount reflecting the fair value of the underlying asset plus the amount of any additional financing provided by the buyer-lessor to the seller-lessee. [IFRS 16.101]. This will be reflected in the statements of cash flows by splitting the proceeds between investing cash flows and financing cash flows. However, there are different approaches as to how this split could be determined. From an economic standpoint, the seller-lessee has sold only its interest in the value of the underlying asset at the end of the leaseback - it has retained its right to use the asset for the duration of the leaseback. Therefore, the amount that would be shown as an investing cash inflow must reflect this. Statement of cash flows How we see it In our view there are two acceptable approaches for determining the split between investing cash flows and financing cash flows. One approach is to present the investing cash inflow as the amount of the sale proceeds that reflects the proportion of the fair value of the underlying asset that is not retained by the seller-lessee. Any excess would be presented as part of cash flows from financing activities. This presentation in the statement of cash flows would be consistent with the requirement in IFRS 16 to recognise only the amount of any gain or loss on the sale and leaseback relating to the rights that have been transferred to the buyer-lessor and also to measure the right-of-use asset as a proportion of the previous carrying amount of the underlying asset. Alternatively, the seller-lessee may also present any excess of the sale proceeds over the fair value of the asset as financing cash inflows and the remaining proceeds as investing cash inflows. This can be seen in Illustration 8-1 below (see also Chapter 24 at section 8.2.4), which uses an example in IFRS 16 as a starting point and further expands on the example to demonstrate the presentation of cash flows in the statement of cash flows. [IFRS 16.IE Example 24]. Illustration 8-1: Sale and leaseback transaction: presentation of cash flows An entity (Seller-lessee) sells a building to another entity (Buyer-lessor) for cash of CU2,000,000. Immediately before the transaction, the building is carried at a cost of CU1,000,000. At the same time, Seller-lessee enters into a contract with Buyer-lessor for the right to use the building for 18 years, with annual payments of CU120,000 payable at the end of each year. The terms and conditions of the transaction are such that the transfer of the building by Seller-lessee satisfies the requirements for determining when a performance obligation is satisfied in IFRS 15. Accordingly, Seller-lessee and Buyer-lessor account for the transaction as a sale and leaseback. This example ignores any initial direct costs. The fair value of the building at the date of sale is CU1,800,000. The amount of the excess sale price of CU200,000 (CU2,000,000 - CU1,800,000) is recognised as additional financing provided by the Buyer-lessor to Seller-lessee. At the commencement date, Seller-lessee measures the right-of-use asset arising from the leaseback of the building at the proportion of the previous carrying amount of the building that relates to the right-of-use retained by Seller-lessee, which is CU699,556. This is calculated as: CU1,000,000 (the carrying amount of the building) ÷ CU1,800,000 (the fair value of the building) × CU1,259,200 (the discounted lease payments for the 18-year right-of-use asset) and represents 70% of the building’s carrying amount. The above example in IFRS 16 does not discuss the presentation of cash flows in the statement of cash flows. In our view, in its statement of cash flows, the Seller-lessee presents financing cash inflows of CU1,459,200 (being the sum of the lease liability of CU1,259,200 and the additional financing provided by the Buyer-lessor of CU200,000) and the remaining CU540,800 (i.e., CU2,000,000 - CU1,459,200) as investing cash inflows. An alternative approach in this example, could be to treat only the excess of any sale proceeds above the fair value of the underlying asset of CU200,000 (i.e., CU2,000,000 - CU1,800,000) as financing cash flows with CU1,800,000 presented within investing activities. 8.5 Accounting as lessor For lessors, IFRS 16 is silent on the presentation of cash flows arising from their leasing activities. However, since lessor accounting under IFRS 16 is substantially unchanged from the accounting under the predecessor standard, IAS 17 Leases, in practice, the presentation of cash flows for lessors should be established and well understood. In summary, the cash inflows from operating leases will form part of the cash flows from operating activities. [IAS 7.14]. The cash inflows from finance leases will follow the treatment set out in section 7.2 above in relation to assets disposed of on deferred terms. Statement of cash flows Additional IAS 7 considerations for groups IAS 7 does not distinguish between single entities and groups, and there are no specific requirements as to how an entity prepares a consolidated statement of cash flows. In the absence of specific requirements, cash inflows and outflows would be treated in the same way as income and expenses under IFRS 10 Consolidated Financial Statements. Applying these principles, the statement of cash flows presented in consolidated financial statements will reflect only the flows of cash and cash equivalents into and out of the group, i.e., consolidated cash flows are presented as those of a single economic entity. [IFRS 10 Appendix A]. On the same basis, the cash flows of a consolidated subsidiary must be included in the consolidated statement of cash flows for the same period as its results are reported in the consolidated statement of comprehensive income, i.e., from the date the group gains control until the date it loses control. [IFRS 10.B88]. Cash flows that are internal to the group (such as payments and receipts for intra-group sales, management charges, dividends, interest and financing arrangements) must be eliminated. [IFRS 10.B86]. However, transactions with non-controlling interests as well as with associates, joint ventures and unconsolidated subsidiaries would not be eliminated and are discussed in greater detail below. 9.1 Preparing a consolidated statement of cash flows In principle, the group statement of cash flows will be compiled from those prepared by individual subsidiaries with intra-group cash flows being eliminated as part of the aggregation process. This would generally be the case for entities presenting operating cash flows under the direct method, where information on gross cash receipts and payments has been obtained from each group entity’s accounting records. In practice, however, it may be possible to prepare a statement of cash flows at a more consolidated level, by starting with the disclosures in the consolidated statement of comprehensive income and statement of financial position and then applying the adjustments reflected as part of the financial statement consolidation process, together with information provided on external cash flows by individual subsidiaries. Thus, an entity adopting the direct method could use this information to derive the value of the major classes of gross cash receipts and gross cash payments. [IAS 7.19]. An entity presenting operating cash flows under the indirect method would use this information to calculate the values for movements in inventories, operating receivables and payables and other non-cash items that appear in the reconciliation of consolidated profit or loss to the group’s cash flow from operating activities. [IAS 7.20]. As noted in section 6.3 above, groups with foreign subsidiaries will need to take extra care when using this method to ensure exchange differences are treated appropriately. Cash flows from investing and financing activities could similarly be derived from a reconciliation of the relevant headings in the consolidated statement of comprehensive income to statement of financial position movements. However, for this to be possible, subsidiaries would have to provide supplementary information (as part of internal group reporting) to prevent gross cash flows from being netted off and to ensure that the cash flows are shown under the correct classifications. In particular, detailed information about receivables and payables would be essential to ensure that the movements in operating, investing and financing receivables and payables are identified. 9.2 Transactions with non-controlling interests Dividends paid to non-controlling interest holders in subsidiaries are included under cash flows from financing activities or operating activities, in accordance with the entity’s determined policy for dividends paid (see section 4.4.1 above). IFRS 10 requires entities to distinguish between transactions that give rise to a change in control and those that do not, because a transaction when there is no change in control is effectively one with the owners in their capacity as owners. [IFRS 10.BCZ168]. Changes in ownership interests in a subsidiary that do not result in a loss of control are therefore accounted for as equity transactions, and the resulting cash flows are classified in the same way as other transactions with owners. [IAS 7.42B]. Accordingly, IAS 7 requires cash flows arising from changes in ownership interests in a subsidiary that occur after control is obtained, but do not give rise to a loss of control to be classified as cash flows from financing activities. [IAS 7.42A]. Statement of cash flows 9.3 Acquisitions and disposals Extract from IAS 7 The aggregate cash flows arising from obtaining or losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses shall be presented separately and classified as investing activities. An entity shall disclose, in aggregate, in respect of both obtaining and losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses during the period each of the following: (a) the total consideration paid or received; (b) the portion of the consideration consisting of cash and cash equivalents; (c) the amount of cash and cash equivalents in the subsidiaries or other businesses over which control is obtained or lost; and (d) the amount of the assets and liabilities other than cash or cash equivalents in the subsidiaries or other businesses over which control is obtained or lost, summarised by each major category. 40A … The separate presentation of the cash flow effects of obtaining or losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses as single line items, together with the separate disclosure of the amounts of assets and liabilities acquired or disposed of, helps to distinguish those cash flows from the cash flows arising from the other operating, investing and financing activities.
