The cash flow effects of losing control are not deducted from those of obtaining control. **[IAS 7.39, IAS 7.40, IAS 7.41].** Cash flows arising from changes in ownership interests in a subsidiary that do not result in a loss of control are classified as financing cash flows (see section 9.2 above). [IAS 7.42A]. Statement of cash flows The aggregate amount of cash paid or received as consideration is reported in the statement of cash flows net of cash and cash equivalents acquired or disposed of. [IAS 7.42]. The cash flow effects of losing control are not deducted from those of gaining control. [IAS 7.41]. This implies that entities must present one analysis for all acquisitions and another for all disposals, such as that presented by Naspers Limited, shown in Practical example 9-1 below. Practical example 9-1: Naspers Limited (2023) South Africa Annual financial statements [extract] Consolidated statement of cash flows [extract] Cash flows from investing activities [extract] … … Notes to the consolidated annual financial statements [extract] Statement of cash flows 9.3.1 Acquisition-related costs IFRS 3 Business Combinations requires acquisition-related costs (other than those costs relating to the issue of equity or debt securities) to be recognised as an expense in the period in which the costs are incurred and the services are received. [IFRS 3.53]. As discussed in section 4.2 above, the definition of investing activities in IAS 7, states that “only expenditures that result in a recognised asset in the statement of financial position” give rise to investing cash flows. [IAS 7.16]. As a result, cash flows relating to acquisition costs recognised as an expense must be classified within operating activities. 9.3.2 Deferred and other non-cash consideration Not all acquisitions or disposals of businesses are satisfied in full by the exchange of cash. Any non-cash consideration, such as shares issued by either party or amounts to be paid or received by the entity at a later date, is not included in the amount presented under investing activities. [IAS 7.43]. Instead, the non-cash element of the acquisition or disposal is disclosed. As explained in more detail in section 7.1 above, the purchase of assets on deferred terms can be a complicated area because it may not be clear whether to classify the associated cash flows within investing activities or within financing activities, as the repayment of borrowings. Hence, judgement is required when determining the appropriate classification of the deferred payment in the statement of cash flows. These same principles apply on the acquisition of a business involving deferred consideration. 9.3.3 Contingent consideration paid on business combinations When a business combination agreement allows for adjustments to the cost of the combination that are contingent on one or more future events, IFRS 3 requires the acquirer to recognise the acquisition-date fair value of the contingent consideration, [IFRS 3.39], and classify an obligation to pay the contingent consideration as a liability or as equity in accordance with the provisions of IAS 32. [IFRS 3.40]. Changes resulting from events after the acquisition date, such as meeting a performance target, are not reflected by adjusting the recorded cost of the business combination. Where a liability has been recognised for the contingent consideration payable, any subsequent payment in excess of or below the carrying amount of the liability is recognised in profit or loss. [IFRS 3.58]. The primary principle for the classification of cash flows is the nature of the activity giving rise to the cash flow, according to the definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in the standard. This might imply that all payments relating to a business combination must be classified as investing cash flows. However, as discussed in section 4.2 above, the Board added a requirement in IAS 7 that only expenditures that result in a recognised asset are eligible for classification as investing activities. [IAS 7.16]. How we see it In our view, cash payments up to the amount recognised for the acquisition-date fair value of the contingent consideration (and thereby included in the carrying value of the acquired assets including goodwill) would be classified in investing activities. Since contingent consideration generally arises for reasons other than the provision of finance to the acquirer, any resulting payments would generally not seem to be eligible for classification as financing cash flows. As a result, any contingent consideration in excess of the amount that was recorded on the acquisition date, would need to be presented as operating cash flows by default. 9.3.4 Contingent consideration conditional on continuing employment As discussed in Chapter 10 at section 11.2, a transaction that remunerates employees or former owners of the acquiree for future services is excluded from the business combination accounting and accounted for separately. [IFRS 3.52]. Therefore, cash flows relating to payment of purchase consideration that is conditional on the continuing employment of the vendors in the business or the existing employees of the business must be classified within operating activities and not within investing activities. This faithfully reflects the fact that these payments are not part of the consideration transferred on a business combination but are remuneration for post-combination services received. [IFRS 3.B55(a)]. Statement of cash flows 9.3.5 Contingent consideration received on disposals When an entity disposes of a subsidiary, it presents the cash flows separately within investing activities. This will be the case even for the receipt of contingent consideration because the cash inflow is still an investing cash flow being the receipt from the sale of equity or debt instruments of another entity (see section 4.2 above). [IAS 7.16]. 9.3.6 Settlement of amounts owed by the acquired entity A question that sometimes arises is how to treat a payment made by the acquirer, in the consolidated statement of cash flows, to settle amounts owed by a newly acquired subsidiary. If the repayment is made before the acquisition date, the acquiree is not yet part of the group controlled by the acquirer and the debt will not be included in the consolidated balance sheet. Therefore, any payment to settle that debt represents a payment made to acquire the debt instrument of another entity and this would be included under investing activities. [IAS 7.16]. If the repayment is made at or after the acquisition date, it may be more challenging to determine whether the cash outflow should be presented as investing or financing. The repayment may be negotiated with the vendor as a condition of the acquisition agreement or may be triggered by a pre-existing change of control clause in the agreement between the acquired entity and the lender. Alternatively, the payment may be made after the acquisition date and not negotiated and agreed as part of the business combination. IAS 7 defines investing activities as “the acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other investments not included in cash equivalents” and financing activities as “activities that result in changes in the size and composition of the contributed equity and borrowings of the entity”. [IAS 7.6]. As discussed in Section 4 above, the primary principle for classification of cash flows should be in accordance with the nature of the activity in a manner that is most appropriate to the business of the entity. [IAS 7.11]. Therefore, depending on the facts and circumstances associated with repayment, the cash outflow may represent either an investing activity or a financing activity in the consolidated cash flow statement of the acquirer. How we see it Judgement will be needed, based on consideration of all the specific facts and circumstances associated with the payment, to determine whether the cash outflow is one that is investing in nature or financing in nature. In our view, in determining the nature of activities associated with the cash outflow, it is important to consider who is requiring or directing the repayment. If the acquirer is directing the movement of the cash because it has made a choice to repay the external borrowings, perhaps because it wants the acquired entity to be debt free or wishes to arrange financing with its own lenders, then it is more likely that the transaction will be financing in nature. However, if the acquirer is forced to repay the borrowings, for example by a pre-existing change of control clause that the external lender has chosen to exercise, then the payment has been triggered by the business combination and is more likely to be investing in nature. It should be noted that the inclusion of a clause in the acquisition agreement requiring the repayment of the loan by the acquirer is not, in itself, justification for presenting the cash outflow as investing. Although this could be viewed as providing the acquirer with no discretion as to whether it makes the repayment, the acquirer would have been able to influence the content of the acquisition agreement and hence, may have had some choice as to whether this clause was included. In this case, other factors would need to be considered before a conclusion is reached that the cash flow is investing in nature. Clear disclosure of the presentation treatment adopted in the statement of cash flows will be important, in particular, disclosure of the facts considered, and the judgements management has made in determining the nature of the cash flow, in accordance with paragraphs 117 and 122 of IAS 1 (see Chapter 3 at section 5.1.1.A). Other factors that may provide supporting evidence as to the nature of the repayment include: • How close is the timing of the repayment to the date of the acquisition and what is the reason for any difference in timing? The longer the period of time elapsed between the acquisition date and the date of repayment, the more likely it is that the payment is financing in nature. However, it will also be necessary to consider the reason for any significant delay. For example, if the timing of the payment is out of the control of the acquirer, such as where this is dictated by the bank through a change of control clause, a difference in timing may not always be indicative of the payment being financing in nature. Statement of cash flows • Why is the payment being made by the acquirer directly to the lender rather to the vendor or the acquiree who will then repay the borrowings? Is it for the convenience of the vendor or for the convenience of the acquirer? If the arrangement has been established purely for the convenience of the acquirer so that they do not need to transfer the cash to the acquiree to repay the borrowings after acquisition, then this suggests that this is part of the financing activities of the group. However, if the vendor has requested that this amount negotiated as part of the acquisition cost be paid directly by the acquirer to the bank, rather than to the vendor who will then repay the borrowings, it is more likely that the cash outflow is investing in nature. If it is determined that the cash outflow is financing in nature, being the repayment of the borrowings of the acquired entity which is now part of the group, using funds provided by the parent, this is presented as a cash outflow from financing activities in the consolidated statement of cash flows. [IAS 7.17]. If it is determined that the cash outflow is investing in nature because it is part of the payment to acquire control of the acquired entity, then it is presented as an investing cash outflow. Care should be taken when considering whether the payment is part of the consideration for the business combination under IFRS 3 as the payment is not made to the vendor. This is because IFRS 3 paragraph 51 explains that amounts that are not part of what the acquirer and vendor exchanged in the business combination, i.e., amounts that are not part of the exchange for the acquiree, must be accounted for as separate transactions (see Chapter 10 at section 11). 9.3.7 Settlement of intra-group balances on a demerger A similarly fine distinction might apply on the demerger of subsidiaries. These sometimes involve the repayment of intra-group indebtedness out of the proceeds from external finance raised by the demerged subsidiary. If the external funding is raised immediately prior to the subsidiary leaving the group, it is strictly a financing inflow in the consolidated statement of cash flows, being cash proceeds from issuing short or long-term borrowings. [IAS 7.17]. If the subsidiary both raises the external funding and repays the intra-group debt after the demerger, the inflow is shown in the consolidated statement of cash flows under investing activities, being a cash receipt from the repayment of advances and loans made to other parties. **[IAS 7.16].** 9.3.8 Acquisition of a business that is not a subsidiary A business combination may be enacted through the acquisition of a subsidiary (i.e., a business held in a separate legal entity) or through the acquisition of a group of assets and liabilities that constitute a business (sometimes referred to as ‘a purchase of net assets and trade’). Paragraphs 39 and 40 of IAS 7 refer to “obtaining or losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses” and hence are applicable to both types of business combination. Therefore, the cash flows arising on the acquisition and disposal of a group of assets and liabilities that constitute a business would be classified as investing activities. In addition, the considerations set out in sections 9.3.1 to 9.3.7 above in respect of deferred and contingent consideration, acquisition costs and settlement of amounts must also be applied. The disclosure requirements in IAS 7.40 would also be required. 9.4 Cash flows of associates, joint arrangements and investment entities 9.4.1 Investments in associates and joint ventures Changes in cash and cash equivalents relating to associates or joint ventures accounted for under the equity or cost method will be reflected in the entity’s statement of cash flows only to the extent of the cash flows between the group and the investee. [IAS 7.37]. The same concept would apply to associates or joint ventures carried at fair value as allowed by IAS 28 Investments in Associates and Joint Ventures (discussed in Chapter 12). Examples include cash dividends received and loans advanced or repaid. [IAS 7.37]. Cash flows in respect of an entity’s investment in an equity accounted associate or joint venture would also be presented. [IAS 7.38]. Statement of cash flows Cash dividends received from equity accounted associates and joint ventures would be classified as operating or investing activities in accordance with the entity’s determined policy for other dividends received (see section 4.4.1 above). Loans advanced or repaid would be classified as investing activities. [IAS 7.16]. Where the net cash inflow from operating activities is determined using the indirect method, the group’s share of profits or losses from equity-accounted investments will appear as a non-cash reconciling item in the cash flow statement (see section 4.1.2 above). The cash flows arising on the acquisition and disposal of an associate or a joint venture would be classified as investing activities. In addition, the considerations set out in section 9.3 above in respect of deferred and contingent consideration on acquisition and disposal of a subsidiary must also be applied when determining the appropriate classifications of cash flows relating to the acquisition and disposal of associates and joint ventures. 9.4.2 Cash flows of joint operations IAS 7 does not specifically deal with the treatment of the cash flows of joint operations. However, following the principles in IFRS 11 Joint Arrangements that all transactions shall be reflected in the accounts of the joint operator’s financial results to the extent of its interests in those transactions, [IFRS 11.20], the cash flows of the joint arrangement are already included in the operator’s financial statements and no additional adjustments are required to reflect the activities of the joint operation. The treatment of cash flows for the acquisition and disposal of a joint operation is less clear as there is an argument for presentation either as a single net cash flow in investing activities (as is required for the cost of a business combination, discussed in section 9.3 above); or as separate cash flows, classified according to the nature of the underlying assets and liabilities acquired. IFRS 11 requires an entity to determine whether the activity undertaken by a joint operation constitutes a business as defined in IFRS 3 and to apply business combination or asset acquisition accounting in accordance with that analysis. [IFRS 11.21A]. How we see it In our view, an entity presents the cash flows for the acquisition and disposal of a joint operation consistently with the treatment of the joint operation as either a business or a group of assets and liabilities as required by IFRS 11.21A. Therefore, acquisitions and disposals of operations meeting the definition of a business would give rise to a single investing cash flow, and acquisitions and disposals of operations not regarded as a business would give rise to cash flows presented according to the nature of the assets and liabilities acquired or disposed. 9.4.3 Cash flows in investment entities IFRS 10 includes an exception to the consolidation principle for a parent that meets the definition of an investment entity. An investment entity measures its investments in subsidiaries, other than those solely providing services that relate to its investment activities, at fair value through profit or loss in accordance with IFRS 9 instead of consolidating those subsidiaries. The investment entity exception is discussed in # Chapter 7 at section 10. IAS 7 does not specifically address the treatment of cash flows related to subsidiaries held at fair value in an investment entity. However, as discussed in section 4.1 above, the nature of the business conducted by the parent will be a relevant factor in determining the appropriate classification of these cash flows as operating, investing or financing. An investment entity has a business purpose to invest solely for capital appreciation, investment income, or both. [IFRS 10.B85B]. Therefore, investing in subsidiaries is part of the main revenue-generating activity of the parent and so any related cash flows are classified as operating activities. The disclosures required by an investment entity on the acquisition of subsidiaries are less than those required for other entities. Investment entities need only disclose the total consideration paid or received and the portion of the consideration consisting of cash and cash equivalents. [IAS 7.40, IAS 7.40A]. Statement of cash flows 9.5 Cash flows in separate financial statements 9.5.1 Cash flows of subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures IAS 7 addresses the treatment of cash flows of associates, joint ventures and subsidiaries accounted for by use of the cost method, restricting its reporting in the statement of cash flows to the cash flows between investor and the investee, for example, to dividends and advances as discussed in section 9.4.1 above. [IAS 7.37]. This treatment would also be applied to associates, joint ventures and subsidiaries either held at fair value through profit or loss or accounted using the equity method in the separate financial statements, as allowed by IAS 27 Separate Financial Statements and discussed in Chapter 9 at section 2. As noted in section 4.1 above, the nature of the business conducted by the entity will be a relevant factor in determining the appropriate classification of cash flow as operating, investing or financing, having regard to the definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in paragraph 6 of IAS 7. This principle applies to the presentation of cash payments and receipts between a parent and its subsidiaries, associates and joint ventures in the cash flow statement which forms part of the separate financial statements of the parent. For example, where a parent provides a loan to a subsidiary, an associate or a joint venture, it will be necessary to consider whether the cash outflow (and the subsequent cash inflow when the loan is repaid) is presented as part of operating activities or investing activities in the separate financial statements of the parent. [IAS 7.13, IAS 7.16]. Conversely, where a subsidiary, an associate or a joint venture provides a loan to the parent, this is much more clearly part of financing activities of the parent. [IAS 7.17]. See sections 4.1 to 4.3 above for details of the definitions of operating, investing and financing activities in IAS 7. 9.5.2 Group cash pooling and cash sharing arrangements Many groups use some form of cash pooling or cash sharing arrangement to manage the group’s cash position on a consolidated basis, effectively concentrating the group’s cash in one place. Such arrangements seek to maximise the return for the group as a whole on its cash, minimise the cost of funding and give visibility to the group of its combined cash and currency position. The terms and conditions of such arrangements vary, and it is important to understand the specific nature of the pooling or sharing arrangement in place, in order to determine the appropriate accounting treatment. In sections 9.5.3 and 9.5.4 below we discuss three of the most common types of arrangements. 9.5.3 Group treasury arrangements Some groups adopt treasury arrangements under which cash resources are held centrally, either by the parent company or by a designated subsidiary company. Any excess cash is transferred to the central bank account which is in the name of the designated ‘treasury entity’. In some cases, each individual group company might not even have its own bank account, with all receipts and payments being made directly from the centrally controlled funds. Each individual group company will need to consider how to present the funds transferred to the treasury entity at the period end; as cash, as cash equivalents or as a short-term intercompany receivable, by examining the specific facts and circumstances of each arrangement with reference to the definitions of cash and of cash equivalents as discussed in sections 3.1 and 3.2 above. In order to meet the definition of cash, the individual company must be able to withdraw the funds from the treasury entity on demand without prior notice being required and without penalty. To meet the definition of cash equivalents, the funds must be held by the individual company for the purpose of meeting short- term cash commitments and be a short-term highly liquid investment that is readily convertible into known amounts of cash and subject to insignificant risk of changes in value. [IAS 7.6, IAS 7.7]. In relation to the definitions of both cash and cash equivalents, one important factor to consider is the liquidity of the treasury entity holding the cash on behalf of the group companies and of the group as a whole. Clearly, if the treasury entity is limited in its ability to “release” cash to other group companies due to liquidity constraints, it will be difficult to justify that the other group companies have a balance that is available for withdrawal on demand (as in the definition of cash) or “highly liquid” (as required by the definition of cash equivalents). [IAS 7.6]. Statement of cash flows When looking at the definition of cash equivalents (see section 3.2 above), the funds deposited also need to be subject to an insignificant risk of change in value. [IAS 7.7]. As explained in section 3.2.2 above, this means that the entity must conclude that the range of possible returns is very small. Therefore, the use of the funds by the treasury entity (e.g., the nature and diversification of the investments made by the treasury entity) as well as the credit risk of the treasury entity and the group as a whole, will be important facts to consider. A further question arises as to whether the subsidiary must prepare a statement of cash flows if there is no cash or cash equivalents balance held at each period end and, in some cases, at any time during the year. How we see it In our view, the preparation of the statement of cash flows will be based upon the actual cash flows during the period regardless of cash and cash equivalents balance held directly by the entity. Where no cash flows through an entity, but rather all transactions flow through the group treasury function acting on behalf of the entity, the entity would still record receipts from debtors and payments to suppliers, albeit with an associated deposit to or withdrawal from a balance with another group company. Just as a bank processes payments and receipts as agent for the account holder, so the group treasury function is acting as agent for the entity, and these transactions will be reflected in a statement of cash flows. For guidance to assist in the determination of whether another entity is acting on behalf of the reporting entity, as the entity’s agent, see section 5.3 above. Where the subsidiary makes net deposits of funds to, or net withdrawals of funds from the designated group entity during the reporting period, a further question arises as to how to present these movements in the subsidiary’s statement of cash flows. As explained above, these transactions normally give rise to intercompany balances. Therefore, the deposits or withdrawals must be evaluated against the definitions of the categories of cash flows and presented as either operating activities or alternatively as investing or financing activities, as appropriate. Further consideration may be made as to whether these cash flows meet the criteria for net presentation as discussed in section 5.2 above. 9.5.4 Cash pooling Groups often use what are commonly known as cash pooling arrangements. Typically, these will involve a number of subsidiaries within a group each having a legally separate bank account with the same bank, but these accounts are managed, with the agreement of the bank, on a consolidated basis. For example, interest will normally be determined on a notional basis using the net balance of all accounts; similarly, any overdraft limit will normally apply to the net balance. Typical characteristics of notional cash pooling and physical cash pooling are discussed below however arrangements vary. Therefore, terms and conditions of each arrangement must be reviewed carefully to ensure that the accounting treatment adopted is appropriate for that specific arrangement. 9.5.4.A Notional cash pooling Notional cash pooling arrangements allow the group to net off the balances of different accounts across jurisdictions, without physically moving the cash to a header or master account, but with each individual entity continuing to have access to the cash it has deposited in its individual account, sometimes called a ‘memo’ or ‘mirror’ account. This is, in essence, an interest rate adjustment, and the ownership of the cash does not change. In such cases, therefore, each entity will continue to report its cash as ‘cash and cash equivalents’. 9.5.4.B Physical cash pooling Typically, in these arrangements, which are also known as zero balancing, cash concentration or physical cash sweeping, the group physically sweeps excess cash out of the individual bank accounts to a ’header account’ on a daily basis. In these arrangements, the ownership of the cash changes: the relationship moves from a deposit with a third-party bank to a loan to the group entity that is the holder of the ’header account’. As in the group treasury arrangements discussed in section 9.5.3 above, the individual companies will need to consider whether to present these balances at the period end as cash, as cash equivalents or as a short-term intercompany receivable. The presentation of cash balances in cash pooling arrangements in the group accounts, in particular whether the individual balances must be presented net or gross, is discussed in Chapter 50 at section 7.4.1.E. Statement of cash flows ## Additional IAS 7 considerations for financial institutions IAS 7 applies to banks, insurance entities and other financial institutions. Nevertheless, there are some differences in its application as compared to entities that are not financial institutions. For example, in considering the components of cash and cash equivalents, banks would not usually have borrowings with the characteristics of an overdraft, and cash for their purposes would normally include cash and balances at central banks, together with loans and advances to other banks repayable on demand. NatWest Group plc discloses such items as components of its cash and cash equivalents, as shown in Practical example 10-1 below. Practical example 10-1: NatWest Group plc (2023) United Kingdom Annual Report and Accounts 2023 [extract] Financial statements [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] … IAS 7 contains a number of additional provisions affecting the preparation of statements of cash flow by financial institutions. These are covered in broad outline below. ### Operating cash flows Cash advances and loans made by financial institutions are usually classified as operating activities (and not as investing activities, as they are for other entities) since they relate to a financial institution’s main revenue-producing activity. [IAS 7.15, IAS 7.16(e)]. Similarly, receipts from the repayment of loans and advances would be included in operating cash flows. [IAS 7.16(f)]. Interest paid and interest and dividends received are usually classified as operating cash flows for a financial institution. [IAS 7.33]. For an insurance entity, cash receipts and cash payments for premiums and claims, annuities and other policy benefits would be included in its operating cash flows as these relate to the principal activities of the entity. Under the direct method of reporting operating cash flows, a financial institution that does not obtain information from its accounting records can derive the disclosures for major classes of gross cash receipts and payments by adjusting interest and similar income and interest expense and similar charges and other items recognised in profit or loss for: (a) Changes during the period in operating receivables and payables (b) Other non-cash items And (c) Other items for which the cash effects are investing or financing cash flows [IAS 7.19] Subject to the differences noted above, the principles for a financial institution presenting operating cash flows under the indirect method are the same as those discussed in section 4.1.2 above for other entities. ### Reporting cash flows on a net basis Cash flows from each of the following activities of a financial institution may be reported on a net basis: (a) Cash receipts and payments for the acceptance and repayment of deposits with a fixed maturity date (b) The placement of deposits with and withdrawal of deposits from other financial institutions And (c) Cash advances and loans made to customers and the repayment of those advances and loans [IAS 7.24] Statement of cash flows ## Disclosures IAS 7 contains a number of specific disclosure requirements to supplement the information that is presented in the statement of cash flows. These are explained below. In addition, disclosure may be needed to explain the judgements management has made during the preparation of the statement of cash flows, especially where the appropriate classification of an item is not clear from IAS 7. IAS 1 requires disclosure of the judgements that management has made that have the most significant effect on the amounts recognised in the financial statements, [IAS 1.122], as well as any information that is relevant to an understanding of any of the entity’s financial statements. [IAS 1.112]. ### Disclosure of cash and cash equivalents IAS 7 paragraphs 45 to 48 include requirements for specific disclosures relating to the composition of cash and cash equivalents. These cover: • The components of cash and cash equivalents and a reconciliation to the amounts in the statement of financial position (see section 11.1.1 below) • The policy adopted in determining the composition of cash and cash equivalents (see section 11.1.2 below) • Details of any change in the policy for determining components of cash and cash equivalents (see section 11.1.3 below) • The amount of significant cash and cash equivalent balances held by the entity that are not available for use by the group (see section 11.1.4 below). 11.1.1 The components of cash and cash equivalents and reconciliation with items in the statement of financial position Cash and cash equivalents is one of the required line items that must be presented on the face of the statement of financial position. [IAS 1.54]. However, items forming part of cash and cash equivalents for IAS 7 purposes may not all be shown as a single line item in the statement of financial position. Therefore, disclosure is required to reconcile the line items in the statement of financial position to the amount of cash and cash equivalents in the statement of cash flows: [IAS 32.42, IAS 7.45] Extract from IAS 7 An entity shall disclose the components of cash and cash equivalents and shall present a reconciliation of the amounts in its statement of cash flows with the equivalent items reported in the statement of financial position. For example, as discussed in section 3.2.4 above, there are circumstances in which bank overdrafts repayable on demand are included as a component of cash and cash equivalents. In the statement of financial position, the bank overdrafts will be presented as financial liabilities, separate from cash, unless a legal right of set off exists. Therefore, a reconciliation will be needed. Keller Group plc provides such a reconciliation below: Practical example 11-1: Keller Group plc (2023) United Kingdom Annual Report and Accounts 2023 [extract] Financial statements [extract] Notes to the consolidated financial statements [extract] Statement of cash flows 11.1.2 The composition of cash and cash equivalents The standard requires disclosure of the policy an entity adopts in determining the composition of cash and cash equivalents. [IAS 7.46]. Where an entity has applied judgement in interpreting the definitions of cash and cash equivalents (see sections 3.1 and 3.2 above) these judgements must be disclosed. An example of this disclosure is given in Example C in the Illustrative Examples accompanying IAS 7. [IAS 7.IE Example C]. If an entity changes the purpose for which it holds certain investments, for example a deposit which was previously held for short term cash management is now held to generate an investment return, this would be a change in facts and circumstances, which must be accounted for prospectively. Similarly, where an entity that previously included its bank overdraft as part of cash and cash equivalents (see section 3.2.4 above) determines that the overdraft no longer forms an integral part of its cash management practices, this must be accounted for prospectively and the overdraft would be reclassified out of cash and cash equivalents and shown as a reconciling item in the cash flow statement. The requirement to present a reconciliation of the amounts in the statement of cash flows is discussed in section 11.1.1 above. 11.1.3 Details of any change in the policy for determining components of cash and cash equivalents If an entity changes its policy for determining components of cash and cash equivalents the effect of the change is reported in accordance with IAS 8. [IAS 7.47]. 11.1.4 The amount of significant cash and cash equivalent balances held by the entity that are not available for use by the group It is possible for a subsidiary of a group to have amounts held in a bank account that meet the definition of cash in respect of the subsidiary but for which use is restricted within the wider group. In these circumstances, IAS 7 requires disclosure of the amount of significant cash and cash equivalent balances held by an entity that are not available for use by the group, together with a commentary by management to explain the circumstances of the restriction. [IAS 7.48]. Examples include cash and cash equivalents held by a subsidiary operating under exchange controls or other legal restrictions that prevent their general use by the parent or other subsidiaries. [IAS 7.49]. In addition, there may be other circumstances that restrict how cash and cash equivalents can be used by an entity. However, IAS 7 does not contain any specific disclosure requirements in respect of such restrictions. In Exposure Draft ED/2014/6 - Disclosure Initiative - Proposed amendments to IAS 7, issued in December 2014, the IASB noted that additional restrictions, such as financial disincentives to utilising certain cash balances, or other considerations may also be relevant to understanding the liquidity of the entity, as they affect the decision to utilise cash and cash equivalents. The example was given of tax liabilities that would arise on the repatriation of foreign cash and cash equivalent balances. The Exposure Draft proposed that where such matters exist, they would need to be disclosed. Although the IASB did not finalise these proposals, disclosure of restrictions must be considered in the context of the liquidity disclosures required by IFRS 7 and more generally, whenever it is material to an understanding of the entity’s financial statements. [IAS 1.112]. IFRS 12 Disclosure of Interests in Other Entities also contains disclosure requirements in consolidated financial statements of the nature and extent of significant restrictions on the group’s ability to access or use assets, and settle liabilities, of its subsidiaries. These disclosures, which are discussed in Chapter 14 at section 4.3, are likely to be relevant for restricted cash and cash equivalent balances held by subsidiaries. ### Changes in liabilities arising from financing activities Extract from IAS 7 44A An entity shall provide disclosures that enable users of financial statements to evaluate changes in liabilities arising from financing activities, including both changes arising from cash flows and non-cash changes. **[IAS 7.44A].** Statement of cash flows Liabilities arising from financing activities are liabilities for which cash flows were, or future cash flows will be, classified in the statement of cash flows as cash flows from financing activities (see section 4.3 above). In addition, the disclosure requirement also applies to changes in financial assets (such as assets that hedge liabilities arising from financing activities) if cash flows from those financial assets were, or future cash flows will be, included in cash flows from financing activities. [IAS 7.44C]. The following changes are required to be disclosed to explain the movements in these instruments: (a) Changes from financing cash flows (b) Changes arising from obtaining or losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses (c) The effect of changes in foreign exchange rates (d) Changes in fair values And (e) Other changes [IAS 7.44B] The standard suggests that these disclosures could be presented in the form of a reconciliation of the opening and closing balances in the statement of financial position for liabilities arising from financing activities. Where such a reconciliation is presented, sufficient information must be provided to enable items included in the reconciliation to be linked to the statement of financial position and the statement of cash flows. [IAS 7.44D]. If an entity provides the disclosure required by IAS 7.44A in combination with disclosures of changes in other assets and liabilities, it must disclose the changes in liabilities arising from financing activities separately from changes in those other assets and liabilities. [IAS 7.44E]. The IFRS IC discussed these requirements further in June 2019.21 In providing the disclosures required by IAS 7.44A, the IFRS IC noted that the requirements of IAS 1 on aggregation and disaggregation would apply (see Chapter 3 at section 4.1.5). Therefore, an entity must disclose any individually material liability (or asset) and any material reconciling item (i.e., cash or non-cash changes) separately in the reconciliation. An entity must also consider whether additional explanation is needed in order to enable users of financial statements to evaluate changes in liabilities arising from financing activities. The disclosures must be structured and disaggregated appropriately to ensure that they: • Provide information about the entity’s sources of finance • Enable investors to check their understanding of the entity’s cash flows • Enable investors to link items to the statement of financial position and the statement of cash flows, or related notes Illustration 11-1 below illustrates how an entity might satisfy the requirement to reconcile liabilities arising from financing activities. The cash flows shown in the example should reconcile to the net of the financing cash inflows and outflows in the statement of cash flows. Illustration 11-1: Reconciliation of liabilities arising from financing activities 20X1 Cash flows Non-cash changes 20X2 Acquisition New leases Exchange differences CU CU CU CU CU CU Bank loans 1,040 - 1,515 Lease liabilities - (90) - Financing liabilities 1,040 2,335 IFRIC Update, June 2019, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. ## Contents - 21. Bank borrowings [extract] Statement of cash flows In Practical example 11-2 below, Inspired PLC also presents the disclosure in a tabular format, but with the categories of liabilities across the top of the table. Practical example 11-2: Inspired PLC (2023) United Kingdom Annual Report & Accounts 2023 [extract] Financial Statements [extract] Notes to the Group financial statements [extract] … … ### Other specific disclosure requirements IAS 7 contains a number of other specific disclosure requirements which have been discussed in sections 4 to 10 above. These are: The total amount of interest paid during the period, whether it has been recognised as an expense or capitalised as part of the cost of an asset. [IAS 7.32]. See section 4.4.1 above. • Disclosures in respect of obtaining and losing control of subsidiaries or other businesses during the period. [IAS 7.40]. See section 9.3 above. • Disclosure of non-cash transaction. [IAS 7.43]. See section 7 above. ### Supplier finance arrangements In May 2023, the IASB published Supplier Finance Arrangements - Amendments to IAS 7 and IFRS 7 to require an entity to provide additional disclosures about its supplier finance arrangements. The amendments are effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2024. The disclosure requirements in the amendments are intended to enable users of financial statements to assess the effects of supplier finance arrangements on an entity’s liabilities, cash flows and exposure to liquidity risk. [IAS 7.44F]. The disclosures aim to enable users to understand how the entity might be affected if the arrangements were no longer available to it [IAS 7.BC29(b)]. The disclosure requirements in the amendments do not apply to an entity’s arrangements that finance receivables or inventory. Also, an entity is not required to identify other actions its suppliers might have taken to finance their receivables (for example, factoring of receivables) [IAS 7.BC32(c)]. The scope of the disclosure requirements is discussed in section 11.4.1 below. The amendments clarify the characteristics of supplier finance arrangements. Extract from IAS 7 44G Supplier finance arrangements are characterised by one or more finance providers offering to pay amounts an entity owes its suppliers and the entity agreeing to pay according to the terms and conditions of the arrangements at the same date as, or a date later than, suppliers are paid. These arrangements provide the entity with extended payment terms, or the entity’s suppliers with early payment terms, compared to the related invoice payment due date. Supplier finance arrangements are often referred to as supply chain finance, payables finance or reverse factoring arrangements. Arrangements that are solely credit enhancements for the entity (for example, financial guarantees including letters of credit used as guarantees) or instruments used by the entity to settle directly with a supplier the amounts owed (for example, credit cards) are not supplier finance arrangements. **[IAS 7.44G].** Statement of cash flows The amendments introduce a number of specific disclosure requirements, covering the terms and conditions of the arrangements, information about the carrying amounts associated with the arrangements and details of the non-cash changes in the carrying amounts. [IAS 7.44H]. Extract from IAS 7 44H To meet the objectives in paragraph 44F, an entity shall disclose in aggregate for its supplier finance arrangements: (a) the terms and conditions of the arrangements (for example, extended payment terms and security or guarantees provided). However, an entity shall disclose separately the terms and conditions of arrangements that have dissimilar terms and conditions. (b) as at the beginning and end of the reporting period: (i) the carrying amounts, and associated line items presented in the entity’s statement of financial position, of the financial liabilities that are part of a supplier finance arrangement. (ii) the carrying amounts, and associated line items, of the financial liabilities disclosed under (i) for which suppliers have already received payment from the finance providers. (iii) the range of payment due dates (for example, 30-40 days after the invoice date) for both the financial liabilities disclosed under (i) and comparable trade payables that are not part of a supplier finance arrangement. Comparable trade payables are, for example, trade payables of the entity within the same line of business or jurisdiction as the financial liabilities disclosed under (i). If ranges of payment due dates are wide, an entity shall disclose explanatory information about those ranges or disclose additional ranges (for example, stratified ranges). (c) the type and effect of non-cash changes in the carrying amounts of the financial liabilities disclosed under (b)(i). Examples of non-cash changes include the effect of business combinations, exchange differences or other transactions that do not require the use of cash or cash equivalents (see paragraph 43) The amendments require an entity to aggregate information about its supplier finance arrangements. This is because, in most cases, aggregated information about an entity’s supplier finance arrangements will satisfy the information needs of users of financial statements. [IAS 7.BC36]. However, an entity must disaggregate information about unusual or unique terms and conditions of individual arrangements when they are dissimilar. [IAS 7.44H]. Explanatory information about payment due dates, when those payment due date ranges are wide, must also be disaggregated. In considering whether, and if so, how to aggregate (or disaggregate) this information, the IASB noted that paragraphs 29 -31 of IAS 1 are relevant when making this assessment. [IAS 7.BC38]. Accordingly, an entity must ensure it provides an appropriate level of disaggregation of information to avoid obscuring material information. The requirements of IAS 1 in respect of materiality and aggregation are discussed in Chapter 3 at section 4.1.5. Each of the required disclosures is discussed in turn from section 11.4.2 below. 11.4.1 Scope of the disclosure requirements Paragraph 44G of IAS 7 explains that supplier finance arrangements are characterised by one or more finance providers offering to pay amounts an entity owes its suppliers and the entity agreeing to pay according to the terms and conditions of the arrangements at the same date as, or a date later than, suppliers are paid. [IAS 7.44G]. While this paragraph clarifies the characteristics of supplier finance arrangements, it does not define which arrangements are within the scope of the disclosure requirements. Therefore, determining which arrangements are in the scope may require judgement. For example, when a purchasing entity is aware that a supplier is factoring its debtors to a third party, and consequently, the entity settles the outstanding balance with the third party directly, would the purchasing entity need to provide the disclosures required by paragraph 44H even if they had not been party to that agreement? The wording of paragraph 44F of IAS 7 requires an entity to “disclose information about its supplier finance arrangements” (emphasis added) which could be read to imply that this only includes arrangements that the entity itself is party to, and not arrangements between other parties. Statement of cash flows However, paragraph 44F also explains that the disclosures are to enable users of financial statements to assess the effects of those arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk which could be interpreted as being wider in scope, possibly covering arrangements that the entity isn’t party to but that could impact its liquidity risk. [IAS 7.44F]. In the Basis for Conclusions to the amendments, it is noted that in developing the requirements, the IASB considered various types of supplier finance arrangements and noted that an entity might enter into arrangements with varying structures and for various reasons. [IAS 7.BC31]. IAS 7.44G defines the arrangements within the scope of the disclosures as being those that provide the entity with extended payment terms, or the entity’s suppliers with early payment terms, compared to the related invoice payment due date. Paragraph BC32(c) explains that the scope of the requirements is confined to arrangements that finance “amounts an entity owes its suppliers” and that an entity is not required to identify other actions its suppliers might have taken to finance their receivables (for example, factoring of receivables). [IAS 7.BC32]. As a result, judgement may be required in order to determine whether certain arrangements are within the scope of the requirements of IAS 7.44F to 44H. How we see it Identifying whether an arrangement is within the scope of the new requirements may involve significant judgement in some circumstances and disclosure of this judgement would be required under IAS 1.122. Care must be taken to assess all relevant facts and circumstances before concluding. It will be relevant to consider the purpose and design of the arrangement with the third-party finance provider, including indicators such as who initiated the scheme, whether it is available to all customers as part of the supplier’s standard terms and conditions or only to the entity and what benefits, if any, the entity obtains from it. This assessment should be performed having regard to the disclosure objective of paragraph 44F which is to “provide information that enables users of financial statements to assess the effects of those arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk.” and how the entity might be affected if the arrangements were no longer available to it. Illustration 11.2 below explains how the requirements might be applied to certain scenarios. However, it should be noted that IAS 1.17(c) requires entities to provide additional information to achieve fair presentation and IAS 1.112(c) requires notes to provide additional information if it is relevant to the understanding of the financial statements (see Chapter 3 at section 3.4 and section 4.1.1.A). Therefore, even if a reporting entity concludes that an arrangement does not contain all the characteristics identified in paragraph 44G of IAS 7, it must still carefully consider the extent to which disclosure of the information specified in paragraphs 44H is relevant to an understanding of its financial statements. Illustration 11-2: Scope of supplier finance arrangements disclosures An entity purchases goods from a supplier at a cost of CU100. Normal industry terms for the purchase would be 3 months payment terms. As a result of the supplier entering into and maintaining a factoring arrangement with a third party, the entity is required to settle the debt directly with the third party and is required to provide certain information (for example half-year reports and annual financial statements) which would allow the third party to assess the credit risk attached to the entity in order to appropriately tailor the terms of the arrangement. Question Is the purchasing entity (‘the entity’) required to provide the supplier finance arrangement disclosures, as envisaged in IAS 7.44F and 44G, in each of the three scenarios below? Scenario 1 - Entity is aware that the supplier is factoring its debtors The entity is aware that the supplier is factoring its debtors to a third party, and consequently, the entity will be required to settle the outstanding balance with the third party directly. The entity is required to settle the invoice of CU100 within the normal industry terms of 3 months. Scenario 2 - Entity obtains payment terms of 6 months by virtue of the supplier’s arrangement The entity is aware that the supplier is factoring its debtors to a third party, and consequently, the entity will be required to settle the outstanding balance with the third party directly. The entity obtains payment terms of 6 months by virtue of the supplier’s arrangement with the third party. The invoiced amount is CU105 to compensate for finance charges. Statement of cash flows Scenario 3 - Entity can opt for the extended terms The purchase agreement between the entity and its supplier includes different payment term options and related costs. At the inception of each purchase, the supplier informs the entity about the possibility of receiving an extension of the due date (from 3 months to 6 months), should the supplier still have the financing facilities available to it through its pre-arranged factoring agreement with the third-party finance provider. If the entity opts for the extended terms, the supplier will issue an invoice for CU105 and 6 month payment terms. If the entity does not opt in, the supplier issues an invoice for CU100 and 3 month payment terms. Analysis Scenario 1 How we see it In the absence of any other relevant facts or contractual conditions, we believe, it would be appropriate for the entity to conclude that the entity is not required to provide supply chain financing disclosure as it is not a party to the arrangement and indeed does not benefit from the arrangement. This is purely an arrangement of the supplier with a third party to factor the supplier’s debtors. As noted in section 11.4.1 above, in the Basis for Conclusions paragraph BC32(c) explains that the scope of the requirements is confined to arrangements that finance “amounts an entity owes its suppliers” and that an entity is not required to identify other actions its suppliers might have taken to finance their receivables (for example, factoring of receivables). The disclosure objective in IAS 7.44F refers to information to enable users of financial statements to assess the effects of those arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk. This arrangement has had no impact on the entity’s liabilities, cash flows or exposure to credit or liquidity risk. This example provides limited facts; entities need to look at all aspects of the arrangement to reach a conclusion. Scenario 2 In this scenario, care must be taken to assess all relevant facts and circumstances before concluding. It will be relevant to consider the purpose and design of the arrangement with the third-party finance provider, including indicators such as who initiated the scheme and whether it is available to all customers as part of the supplier’s standard terms and conditions or only to the entity. This assessment should be performed having regard to the disclosure objective of paragraph 44F which is to “provide information that enables users of financial statements to assess the effects of those arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk.” How we see it In the absence of any other relevant facts or contractual conditions, we believe, it would be consistent with the disclosure objective in paragraph 44F to consider that such arrangement is part of the entity’s supplier finance arrangements. This is because, the entity is allowed to pay an amount greater than the invoice amount, at a date later than the related invoice date. This example provides limited facts; entities need to look at all aspects of the arrangement to reach a conclusion. Scenario 3 In this scenario, care must be taken to assess all relevant facts and circumstances before concluding. It will be relevant to also consider the purpose and design of the arrangement with the third-party finance provider, including indicators such as who initiated the scheme and whether it is available to all customers as part of the supplier’s standard terms and conditions or only to the entity. This assessment should be performed having regard to the disclosure objective of paragraph 44F which is to “provide information that enables users of financial statements to assess the effects of those arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk.” Statement of cash flows How we see it In our view, in the absence of any other relevant facts or contractual conditions, we believe that it would be consistent with the disclosure objective in paragraph 44F to consider that such arrangement is part of the entity’s supplier finance arrangements. This is because, the optional extended payment terms and related costs offered by the supplier are explicitly linked to the financing facilities available to the supplier through its pre-arranged factoring agreement with the third-party finance provider. If the entity chooses to utilise the extended payment terms, the entity becomes party to an arrangement in which the supplier is paid (by the finance provider) at an earlier date, than the date the entity settles its payment obligation towards the finance provider. Paragraph 44F explains that the disclosures are to enable users of financial statements to assess the effects of supplier finance arrangements on the entity’s liabilities and cash flows and on the entity’s exposure to liquidity risk. If the arrangement between the supplier and finance provider was to cease to exist, the purchasing entity’s liquidity would be affected. This example provides limited facts; entities need to look at all aspects of the arrangement to reach a conclusion. 11.4.2 Disclosure of the terms and conditions of the arrangements This disclosure is to inform users of the financial statements that such arrangements are in place and to explain their nature. As noted above, an entity must disaggregate information about terms and conditions of significant individual arrangements when they are dissimilar. This may include the extended payment terms, security or guarantees provided, and any other terms and conditions relevant to meeting the disclosure objective of enabling users of the financial statements to assess the impact on the entity’s liabilities, cash flows and liquidity risk. [IAS 7.44F, IAS 7.44H(a)]. 11.4.3 Disclosure of carrying amounts The amendments require an entity to provide quantitative information about the carrying amount of supplier finance arrangement financial liabilities and the line items in the statement of financial position in which those liabilities are presented. This will include separate disclosure of the amount of the liabilities where the supplier has already taken payment from the third part finance provider. [IAS 7.44H(b)(i), IAS 7.44H(b)(ii)]. This information is required as at the beginning and end of each reporting period. [IAS 7.44H(b)]. See section 11.4.6 below for discussion of transitional reliefs that apply in the first year of application. Illustration 11-3 below, which is not part of the amendments but is based on the example in the IASB’s “Investor Perspectives: Supplier finance - New disclosure requirements to aid investors” document, 22 shows one possible way of how a company might satisfy the requirements of IAS 7.44H(b)(i)and (ii): Illustration 11-3: Carrying amount of liabilities that are part of supplier finance arrangements Carrying amount of liabilities 20X2 20X1 CU CU Presented within trade and other payables 1,500 1,000 Of which suppliers have received payment 1,000 Presented within finance payables 1,000 Of which suppliers have received payment Investors Perspectives - Supplier finance: new disclosure requirements to aid investors, May 2023, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows 11.4.4 Disclosure of the range of payment due dates An entity is required to disclose the range of payment due dates (for example, 30 to 40 days after the invoice date) for payables within the supplier finance arrangements and for comparable trade payables. The standard includes an example of what is meant by “comparable trade payables”, which would be trade payables with the same line of business or jurisdiction, but which have not been included in any supply finance agreements. When those payment due date ranges are wide, explanatory information about payment due dates, or additional ranges, must be provided. [IAS 7.44H(b)(iii)]. This information is required as at the beginning and end of each reporting period. [IAS 7.44H(b)]. See section 11.4.6 below for discussion of transitional reliefs that apply in the first year of application. Illustration 11-3 below, which is not part of the amendments but is based on the example in the IASB’s “Investor Perspectives: Supplier finance - New disclosure requirements to aid investors” document, 23 shows one possible way of how a company might satisfy the requirements of IAS 7.44H(b)(iii): Illustration 11-4: Range of payment due dates Range (days) of payment due dates 20X2 20X1 Liabilities that are part of the arrangement 85-90 days after invoice date 80-90 days after invoice date Comparable trade payables that are not part of an arrangement 60-70 days after invoice date 60-65 days after invoice date 11.4.5 Disclosure of the type and effect of non-cash changes The amendments require disclosure of the type and effect of non-cash changes in the carrying amounts of the financial liabilities that are part of a supplier finance arrangement. These non-cash changes include the effect of business combinations, exchange differences or other transactions that do not require the use of cash or cash equivalents. [IAS 7.44H(c)]. Disclosure of this information enables users of financial statements to assess the carrying amounts of financial liabilities on a more comparable basis from one reporting period to the next and to understand how these non-cash changes affect the entity’s cash flows. [IAS 7.BC37]. See section 4.4.6 above for discussion of the presentation of cash flows arising from supplier finance arrangements, including consideration of when cash payments made on behalf of the reporting entity should be reflected in the entity’s cash flow statement. 11.4.6 Effective date and transitional arrangements The amendments are effective for annual reporting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2024. [IAS 7.62]. The amendments contain some transitional reliefs from disclosures in the first year of application, meaning that in the annual reporting period in which the amendments are first applied comparative information is not required. In addition, no disclosures are required in the first year of application of the opening amounts for payables for which the suppliers have already received payment from finance providers or for the range of payment due dates. [IAS 7.63]. However, the requirement to provide quantitative information about the carrying amount of supplier finance arrangement financial liabilities and the line items in the statement of financial position in which those liabilities are presented (see section 11.4.2 above) is not subject to this transitional relief. As a result, this information will need to be disclosed at the beginning of that first period as well as at the end, meaning that an entity will need to ensure that it has collected the necessary information to enable it to provide these disclosures. For example, an entity with a 31 December reporting date will be required to apply the amendments for the first time in the annual reporting period ending 31 December 2024. The disclosure of supplier finance arrangement financial liabilities and the line items in the statement of financial position in which those liabilities are presented will be required as at 1 January 2024 and at 31 December 2024. [IAS 7.44H(b)(i)]. Investors Perspectives - Supplier finance: new disclosure requirements to aid investors, May 2023, available on the IFRS Foundation’s website. Statement of cash flows The amendments clarify that entities are not required to provide disclosures according to the new requirements in any interim reporting period during the first annual reporting period in which the amendments apply. [IAS 7.63]. ### Voluntary disclosures IAS 7 sets out a number of disclosures which are encouraged but which are not mandatory. [IAS 7.50]. Extract from IAS 7 Additional information may be relevant to users in understanding the financial position and liquidity of an entity. Disclosure of this information, together with a commentary by management, is encouraged and may include: (a) the amount of undrawn borrowing facilities that may be available for future operating activities and to settle capital commitments, indicating any restrictions on the use of these facilities; (b) [deleted] (c) the aggregate amount of cash flows that represent increases in operating capacity separately from those cash flows that are required to maintain operating capacity; and (d) the amount of the cash flows arising from the operating, investing and financing activities of each reportable segment (see IFRS 8 Operating Segments). Although these disclosures are only encouraged in IAS 7, disclosure of the amount of undrawn borrowing facilities that may be available must be considered in the context of the liquidity disclosures required by IFRS 7 and more generally, whenever it is material to an understanding of the entity’s financial statements. [IAS 1.112]. (See Chapter 3 at section 5.1). 11.5.1 Cash flows to increase and maintain operating capacity IAS 7 does not contain any guidance as to how to distinguish cash flows for expansion from cash flows for maintenance in relation to the voluntary disclosure referred to under (c) above. The standard merely states that this information is useful in helping the user to determine whether the entity is investing adequately in the maintenance of its operating capacity or whether it may be sacrificing future profitability for the sake of current liquidity and distributions to owners. [IAS 7.51]. Hongkong Land Holdings Limited distinguishes renovations expenditure from developments capital expenditure in its analysis of investing cash flows. Practical example 11-3: Hongkong Land Holdings Limited (2023) Bermuda Annual Report 2023 [extract] Financial Statements [extract] Consolidated Cash Flow Statement [extract] … … Statement of cash flows 11.5.2 Segment cash flow disclosures Disclosure of segmental cash flows is encouraged because it reveals the availability and variability of cash flows in each segment and allows users to understand the relationship between the cash flows of the business as a whole and those of its component parts. [IAS 7.52]. However, it should be noted that IFRS 8 Operating Segments does not require any segmental cash flow disclosures. See Chapter 32 for guidance on IFRS 8. IAS 7 is accompanied by an example of the segmental disclosure. [IAS 7.IE Example A part D]. However, this example simply reports the operating, investing and financing cash flows of its two segments with no reconciliation of the total to the statement of cash flows. In practice, it might be difficult to allocate financing cash flows across the entity’s reportable segments, given that this may not be how treasury functions tend to operate. A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S provided an analysis of operating cash flows and capital expenditure (part of its investing cash flows) by reportable segment. The entity did not disclose financing cash flows by reportable segment (comparative information is provided in the financial statements but is not reproduced here). Practical example 11-4: A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S (2017) Denmark ## Annual Report [extract] Financials [extract] Consolidated financial statements 2017 [extract] Notes 1 Segment information [extract] … … ## Requirements of other standards ### Cash flows of discontinued operations IFRS 5 Non-current Assets Held for Sale and Discontinued Operations requires an entity to disclose information on the net cash flows of discontinued operations. [IFRS 5.33]. Extract from IFRS 5 An entity shall disclose: (a) … (c) the net cash flows attributable to the operating, investing and financing activities of discontinued operations. These disclosures may be presented either in the notes or in the financial statements. These disclosures are not required for disposal groups that are newly acquired subsidiaries that meet the criteria to be classified as held for sale on acquisition (see paragraph 11). (d) … These disclosures can be presented either on the face of the statement of cash flows or in the notes. Disclosure is not required for disposal groups that are newly acquired subsidiaries which are classified as held for sale on acquisition in accordance with IFRS 5. [IFRS 5.33(c)]. The general presentation requirements of IFRS 5 are dealt with in Chapter 5. IFRS 5 does not amend the requirements of IAS 7.10 to present cash flows classified by activity - see section 4 above. Cash flows from discontinued operations must be analysed amongst each of the three categories. However, the disclosures required by IFRS 5 can be provided in different ways, since IFRS 5 permits presentation on the face of the statement of cash flows or in the notes. In the Practical example below, Netcare Limited elected to show the cash flows of discontinued operations on the face of the statement of cash flows. In the notes to the financial statements, Netcare Limited further analyses these cash flows by separate major business line. Statement of cash flows Practical example 12-1: Netcare Limited (2012) South Africa ## ANNUAL INTEGRATED REPORT [extract] Financials [extract] Annual financial statements [extract] ## Contents - 12. Discontinued operations and assets held for sale [extract] Statement of cash flows NOTES TO THE GROUP ANNUAL FINANCIAL STATEMENTS [extract] ### Discontinued operations [extract] ## Contents - 46. Discontinued operations and non-current assets held for sale [extract] … In contrast, Pennon Group plc presents the information required by IFRS 5 in the notes to the financial statements with no analysis of continuing and discontinued operations on the face of the statement of cash flows. Practical example 12-2: Pennon Group plc (2020) United Kingdom Annual Report & Accounts 2020 [extract] Financial statements [extract] Notes to the financial statements [extract] … … ### Cash flows arising from the exploration of mineral resources IFRS 6 Exploration for and Evaluation of Mineral Resources requires that an entity discloses the amounts of operating and investing cash flows arising from the exploration for and evaluation of mineral resources. [IFRS 6.24(b)]. The requirements of IFRS 6 are discussed in Chapter 39. The requirement for investing cash flows to give rise to the recognition of an asset, discussed in section 4.2 above, is particularly relevant to entities applying IFRS 6. ### Cash flows arising from interests in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates IFRS 12 requires an entity to disclose in its consolidated financial statements summarised financial information about the cash flows for each subsidiary that has non-controlling interests that are material to the entity. [IFRS 12.B10(b)]. These amounts are stated before inter-company eliminations. [IFRS 12.B11]. For each material joint venture and associate, an entity is also required to disclose dividends received from the joint venture or associate [IFRS 12.B12(a)] and, for joint ventures, the amount of cash and cash equivalents. [IFRS 12.B13(a)]. In addition, the standard requires disclosure of any significant restrictions on the ability of joint ventures or associates to transfer funds to the entity in the form of cash dividends, or to repay loans or advances made by the entity. [IFRS 12.22(a)]. IFRS 12 is discussed in Chapter 14. Statement of cash flows Appendix A - Index of Illustrations Illustration 3-1: Investments with a maturity of greater than three months Illustration 3-2: Investment which is not held to meet short term cash commitments Illustration 4-1: Cash flows from derivatives not designated as hedges Illustration 8-1: Sale and leaseback transaction: presentation of cash flows Illustration 11-1: Reconciliation of liabilities arising from financing activities Illustration 11-2: Scope of supplier finance arrangements disclosures Illustration 11-3: Carrying amount of liabilities that are part of supplier finance arrangements Illustration 11-4: Range of payment due dates Statement of cash flows Appendix B - Defined terms Extract from IAS 7 The following terms are used in this Standard with the meanings specified: Cash comprises cash on hand and demand deposits. Cash equivalents are short-term, highly liquid investments that are readily convertible to known amounts of cash and which are subject to an insignificant risk of changes in value. Cash flows are inflows and outflows of cash and cash equivalents. Operating activities are the principal revenue-producing activities of the entity and other activities that are not investing or financing activities. Investing activities are the acquisition and disposal of long-term assets and other investments not included in cash equivalents. Financing activities are activities that result in changes in the size and composition of the contributed equity and borrowings of the entity.
